Itegories
Robin Cockett, Jean-Simon Pacaud Lemay

Abstract
An itegory is a restriction category with a Kleene wand. Cockett, Díaz-Boïls, Gallagher, and Hrubeš briefly introduced Kleene wands to capture iteration in restriction categories arising from complexity theory. The purpose of this paper is to develop in more detail the theory of Kleene wands and itegories. A Kleene wand is a binary operator which takes in two disjoint partial maps, one of type X → X {X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.99998pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X} and the other of type X → A {X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.99998pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A} , and produces a partial map X → A X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.99998pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A . This latter map is interpreted as iterating the endomorphism until it lands in the domain of definition of the second map. In a setting with infinite disjoint joins, there is always a canonical Kleene wand realizing this intuition. The standard categorical interpretation of iteration is via trace operators on coproducts. For extensive restriction categories, we explain in detail how having a Kleene wand is equivalent to this standard interpretation of iteration. This suggests that Kleene wands can be used to replace parametrized iteration and traces in restriction categories which lack coproducts. Further evidence of this is exhibited by providing a matrix construction which embeds an itegory into a traced extensive restriction category. We also consider Kleene wands in classical restriction categories and show how, in this case, a Kleene wand is completely determined by its endomorphism component.

The authors would also like to thank Chad Nester for useful preliminary discussions and his support of this project, also thank Ben MacAdam for reminding the authors about the first named author’s previous work with Díaz-Boïls, Gallagher, and Hrubeš in
[
13
]
. The authors are also grateful to the anonymous reviewers for their useful comments and suggestions. For this research, the first named author was partially funded by NSERC, while the second named author was financially supported by a JSPS PDF (P21746).

We would like to dedicate this paper to Phil Scott (1947–2023).

From Robin:

I have so many memories of time spent with Phil that it is hard to know where to start. In the early 1990s Phil was instrumental in getting me an introduction at the University of Calgary and whence the job I have enjoyed since then. Even back then – in fact, maybe, especially back then – Phil was highly influential and respected. The book with Lambek, “Introduction to higher order categorical logic”, had truly changed the categorical world.

Phil loved visiting Edinburgh, and in 2018 he conspire to have us spend sabbatical time there together. My wife, Polly, and I had long wanted to hike the West Highland Way (which starts near Glasgow). Doing this before our Edinburgh sojourn seemed like a perfect moment. I was therefore to meet my wife, who would be returning from adventures further north, at the start of the hike. Somehow, I needed to leave, in Edinburgh as I passed through on trains from Oxford, a bag of stuff not needed on the hike. Phil concocted a simple logistical plan: as he would already be in Edinburgh, he would meet my midday train at the Edinburgh railway station: I would hand-off my bag to him and hop back on the train to Glasgow.

Well, of course, everything went wrong. The night before the line to Edinburgh from England had been subject to major tree falls due to a storm. When a train finally got through – in the late evening – it was packed with unhappy passengers standing like sardines in the aisles. As Phil did not believe in cell phones, there was no way to let him know how badly delayed I was. When the train finally arrived at Edinburgh the passengers literally poured onto the platform. This included me with a huge suitcase (on wheels) which I had been supposed to hand-off over six hours earlier. I had no idea what I was now going to do with the bag!!

Imagine, however, my disbelief as clinging to the hand-rail at the bottom of the exit stairway, being rudely buffeted by the frustrated crowd pouring forth from the train, stood Phil and his wife Marcia. Relief hardly does justice to my reaction. Unbelievably they had waited for over six hours for a train to get through!

The hand-off went smoothly … and the sabbatical was a special and memorable one!

Phil was hugely generous (and reliable!) … and a friend I sorely miss.

From JS:

Phil was both a friend and a mentor. Phil was an important figure in the early stages of my academic studies and had quite an impact on the direction of my research interests. Phil was my first math professor in undergrad at the University of Ottawa: he was the one who taught me how to do proper proofs. I took many classes from him in undergrad, and Phil quickly became my favourite lecturer. Near the end of undergrad when I was trying to decide what area of research to study, Phil was the one who first mentioned category theory to me by introducing me to star-autonomous categories and Linear Logic. I credit Phil for why and how I became a category theorist. After my time at the University of Ottawa, Phil continued to be a mentor: he was always very supportive and happy to discuss my latest research interests or new results. But more importantly, Phil became a friend. We would often catch up at conferences or whenever I was passing through Ottawa again. We also both visited Edinburgh at the same time, and often went on hikes together. Phil’s passing is a great loss for many communities, including the Canadian mathematics community and the category theory community. Phil was incredibly kind, a fantastic mentor, and a great friend to many of us. Condolences to his loved ones. May he rest in peace.

1
Introduction

Traced monoidal categories were introduced by Joyal, Street, and Verity in
[
34
]
. A traced monoidal category is a braided (though often symmetric in practice) monoidal category equipped with a
trace operator
, which is an operator
\mathsf{Tr}
that takes in a map of type
f:X\otimes A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X\otimes B
, which has a same object
X
in both the domain and codomain, and produces a map of type
\mathsf{Tr}(f):A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
called the
trace
of
f
, where we say that we
traced out
the object
X
. Trace operators can often intuitively be thought of as providing feedback, where
\mathsf{Tr}(f)
can be interpreted as looping back the output
X
into the input
X
of
f
repeatedly. This is why in the graphical calculus for traced monoidal categories
[
36
, Sec 5]
, one draws the trace of
f
by connecting the output wire
X
back into the input wire for
X
creating a cycle. Thus, graphically speaking, having a trace operator allows for loops.

Traced monoidal categories are now very well studied, being an important framework in both mathematics and computer science, with a wide variety of different kinds of examples. In
[
1
]
, Abramsky characterized two kinds of trace monoidal categories: wave style traces and particle style traces. On the one hand, wave style trace can be understood as passing information in a global information wave throughout the system. Examples of wave style traces can be found in
[
27
, Ex 4.5]
, which include
compact closed categories

[
34
, Prop 3.1]
, where the trace operator captures the (partial) trace of matrices and is fundamental in categorical quantum foundations
[
2
]
, and
traced Cartesian monoidal categories

[
37
, Sec 6.4]
, which are traced monoidal categories on the product – in this case trace operator captures the notion of feedback via fixed points
[
29
, Thm 3.1]
. On the other hand, particle style traces are interpreted by streams of particles or tokens flowing around a network. Examples of particle style traces can be found in
[
27
, Ex 4.6]
. Often times, particle style traces can be expressed using partially defined infinite sum operations, and so particle style traces are also often referred to as sum style traces as well. Haghverdi and Scott were particularly interested in these sum style traces, which led to the development of
traced unique decomposition categories

[
24
]
to help better understand these infinite sum formulas for wave style traces. Haghverdi and Scott, along with Abramsky, in a series of papers
[
24
,
3
,
27
,
25
,
26
,
28
]
used traced unique decomposition categories to give deeper analysis and recapture models of Girard’s Geometry of Interaction
[
22
]
.

Most models of particle style traces are in fact
traced coCartesian monoidal categories

[
37
, Sec 6.4]
, which are traced monoidal categories whose monoidal product is a coproduct, where in this case the trace operator captures the notion of feedback instead via
iteration
. It turns out, thanks to the couniversal property of the coproduct, that to give a trace operator on a coproduct
+
is equivalent to giving a
parametrized iteration operator

[
37
, Prop 6.8]
, which is an operator which takes in a map of type
f:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X+A
, and produces a map of type
\mathsf{Iter}(f):X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
, called the
iteration
of
f
. As noted by Selinger
[
37
, Remark 6.10]
, parametrized iteration operators were first defined by Căzănescu and Ungureanu in
[
11
]
as an algebraic theory with iteration. Parametrized iteration operators, and hence traced coCartesian monoidal categories, and other categorial formulations of iteration have been extensively studied by Căzănescu and Ştefănescu
[
10
,
9
]
, Bloom and Ésik
[
7
]
, Adámek and Milius and Velebil
[
4
,
6
,
5
]
along with Goncharov and Rauch
[
23
]
, Jacobs
[
33
]
, and many others.

One of the main examples of a traced coCartesian monoidal category is the category of sets and partial functions. Here, the coproduct is given by disjoint union, while the parametrized iteration operator and trace operator are given by the natural feedback operator. So for a partial function
f:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X+A
,
f(x)
can be thought of landing in either
X
or
A
, but not both, or is undefined. So
\mathsf{Iter}(f):X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is defined as follows. Step 1: take
x
and check if
f(x)
is defined, if not
\mathsf{Iter}(f)(x)
is undefined. Step 2: if
f(x)
is defined and if
f(x)\in A
, then
\mathsf{Iter}(f)(x)=f(x)
. Step 3: if
f(x)\in X
instead, then go back to Step 1 and repeat this process starting from
f(x)
instead of
x
. This process continues until it stops after a finite number of steps: if it does not stop then
\mathsf{Iter}(f)(x)
is undefined. The trace operator is defined via a similar process, where essentially for a partial function
f:X+A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X+B
, its trace continuously iterates the partial function if it continues to land in
X
and only terminates if after a finite number of iterations it eventually lands in
B
. Thus the trace operator and the parametrized iteration operator are forms of
guarded
iteration, that is, an iteration that continues until a certain necessary condition, called the guard, is satisfied.

The trace operator for partial functions is not only a particle style trace, but really a sum style trace, where the partial infinite sum operation is given by the join of partial functions. In fact, partial functions (more precisely partial injections) was one of the main motivating examples that led Haghverdi and Scott to studying traced unique decomposition categories. More precisely, it turns out that partial functions are in fact an example of Arbib and Manes’ notion of a
partially additive category

[
35
]
, which is canonically a traced unique decomposition category that is also a traced coCartesian monoidal category
[
24
, Prop 3.1.4]
. Partial functions are also the canonical example of a
restriction category
. This paper is motivated by the desire to have a restriction category perspective and explanation for the trace operator on partial functions. More precisely, we are interested in studying trace operators for
extensive
restriction categories.

Restriction categories were introduced by the first named author and Lack in
[
17
]
, and they provide the categorial foundations for the theory of partiality. Briefly, a restriction category (Def
2.1
) is a category equipped with a
restriction operator
which associates to every map
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
, an endomorphism
\overline{f}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
called its
restriction
, which captures the domain of definition of
f
. Thus maps in a restriction category can be thought of as partial in an appropriate sense. Restriction categories are now a well established field of research and have been used to capture various important notions related partiality. In
[
19
]
, the first named author and Lack studied extensive restriction categories (Def
7.3
) which, as the name suggests, are the appropriate restriction category analogue of (total) extensive categories
[
8
]
. Briefly, an extensive restriction category is a restriction category with
restriction coproducts
(Def
7.1
), which are coproducts compatible with the restriction, and such that every map has a
decision
(Def
7.4
). For a map
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B_{1}+\ldots+B_{n}
, its decision
\langle f\rangle:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A+\ldots+A
splits up the domain of definition of
f
into the parts that fall into each
B_{i}
respectively. Thus, in an extensive restriction category, the restriction coproduct
+
should indeed be thought of as being a disjoint union. Importantly, this allows extensive restriction categories to have a matrix calculus
[
19
]
, that is, a map of type
A_{1}+\ldots+A_{n}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B_{1}+\ldots+B_{m}
can be represented as an
n\times m
matrix, and where composition is given by matrix multiplication (Sec
8
).

Since restriction coproducts are just coproducts in the usual sense, one can consider trace operators and parametrized iteration operators for restriction coproducts, and thus also consider
traced
extensive restriction categories (Def
9.7
). In particular, the matrix calculus allows us to interpret the trace operator as acting on
2\times 2
square matrices and the parametrized iteration operator as acting on
1\times 2
row matrices, as well as re-expressing their axioms using matrices (Lemma
9.8
). Thus a map of type
X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X+A
is actually a
1\times 2
row matrix consisting of an endomorphism
{X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X}
and a map
X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
. Thus, for an extensive restriction category, a parametrized iteration operator is really an operator that takes an endomorphism
X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X
and a map
X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
which are “disjoint” in an appropriate sense, and produces a map of type
{X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A}
. This observation was made by the first named author, Díaz-Boïls, Gallagher, and Hrubeš in
[
13
]
, where they considered parametrized iteration operators for
distributive
restriction categories with the objective of constructing categories of “timed sets” corresponding to complexity classes. This led to the definition of a
Kleene wand
operator
[
13
, Sec 4]
.

However, Kleene wands were only briefly introduced in
[
13
]
, and only in the context of distributive extensive restriction categories. These have
restriction products
which not all extensive restriction categories have. Moreover, a full proof that a Kleene wand was equivalent to a trace operator, was not provided. This omission is corrected here: we show that, for extensive restriction categories, Kleene wands do provide an equivalent way of describing parametrized iteration and hence a trace operator (Thm
9.11
).

While
[
13
]
was successful in developing categories of timed sets for various complexity classes, Kleene wands themselves were not further developed and, indeed, seemed to have fallen somewhat into obscurity. With the recent interest of studying trace operators in restriction categories, it seems like a good time to dust off Kleene wands. Thus the objective of this paper is to revisit Kleene wands (Def
5.1
), and to study them as a stand-alone concept (Sec
5
). The “advantage” of a Kleene wand is that it can be defined in a setting without coproducts, and therefore can be used to provide a more general notion of iteration.

As explained above, a key feature that makes the trace operator for partial functions work is that the coproduct is given by disjoint union. To capture this in a setting without coproducts, we introduce the concept of an
interference relation
(Def
3.1
), which is a way of describing when maps in a restriction category are disjoint. In particular, maps that are disjoint via an interference relation do have disjoint domains of definition, so when one is defined the other is undefined and vice-versa. We call a restriction category with a chosen interference relation an
interference restriction category
(Def
3.7
). Then a Kleene wand
\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}
on an interference restriction category takes in a map
f:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X
and a map
g:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
which are disjoint and produces a new map
f\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}g:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
. This new map
f\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}g
should be interpreted as iterating
f
until it falls in the domain of definition
g
, in other words, iterating
f
until the guard
g
is satisfies. Thus, in an interference restriction category, a Kleene wand
\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}
is a form of guarded iteration and we call an interference restriction category with a Kleene wand an
itegory
.

We also show how
disjoint joins
(Def
4.1
) can be defined in a general interference restriction category. Then show that, in a setting with countable disjoint joins, there is a canonical Kleene wand given by taking the disjoint join of all iterations of
f
composed with
g
(Thm
5.16
). We also explain how in a
classical
restriction category
[
21
]
– a setting where we have complements (Def
6.1
) – to give a Kleene wand is equivalent to giving a
Kleene upper-star
(Def
6.6
), which is an operator on endomorphisms only.

Every extensive restriction category has a canonical interference relation (Thm
7.10
) given by
separating decisions
(Def
7.11
). Intuitively, two maps in an extensive restriction category are disjoint if there is a decision which allows us to separate their domains of definition. This allows us to show that, for an extensive restriction category, every Kleene wand induces a parametrized iteration operator (Prop
9.9
) and, conversely, every iteration operator induces a Kleene wand (Prop
9.10
). Furthermore, these constructions are inverses of each other. Thus, for an extensive restriction category, a Kleene wand and a parametrized iteration operator are essentially the same thing. Thus, for an extensive restriction category, being traced is equivalent to being an itegory (Thm
9.11
). We also note that there is a matrix construction on an interference restriction category which can be used to obtain an extensive restriction category (Prop
8.2
). Indeed, applying this matrix construction to an itegory we obtain a traced extensive restriction category (Prop
9.15
).

It is worth mentioning that developing interference relations and studying Kleene wand independently, brought to light an important subtlety of Kleene wands which had been overlooked. In
[
13
]
, Kleene wands were only considered for the
maximal
interference relation (Lemma
3.6
), which says that two maps are disjoint if, where one is defined, the other is not, and vice-versa. However, for an extensive restriction category, the canonical interference relation may not necessarily be the maximal interference relation, and it is for the canonical one that correspondence between parametrized iteration operators and Kleene wands hold. Fortunately, the main restriction categories of interest in
[
13
]
were distributive extensive restriction categories with joins and for these the canonical interference relation and the maximal interference relation coincide. Thus, in this case, Kleene wands for the maximal interference relation do indeed correspond to parametrized iteration operators. In this paper, however, we study trace operators for arbitrary extensive restriction categories, so the axioms of a Kleene wand had to be reworked slightly.

Outline and Contributions:

In Sec
2
, we review the basics of restriction category theory and introduce our two main running examples: the category of partial functions and the category of partial recursive functions.

In Sec
3
, we introduce the concept of an interference relation (Def
3.1
) and of an interference restriction category (Def
3.7
). We show how giving an interference relation can be defined on parallel maps or equivalently on restriction idempotents (Prop
3.4
). We prove various useful facts about interference relations (Lemma
3.5
), and also show that there is always a maximal and a minimal interference relation (Lemma
3.6
).

In Sec
4
, we adapt the notion of disjoint joins to interference restriction categories (Def
4.1
). We explain what it means for an interference restriction category to have all finite disjoint joins (Lemma
4.5
) and give a construction which embeds an interference restriction category into a disjoint interference restriction category (Prop
4.10
).

In Sec
5
, we introduce Kleene wands for interference restriction categories, generalizing the definition slightly from
[
13
]
from the maximal interference relation to any arbitrary interference relation. As mentioned above, we call interference restriction categories with disjoint joins and a Kleene wand an itegory. We provide various interesting identities that all Kleene wands satisfy (Lemma
5.2
,
5.3
, &
5.7
). We also consider the notion of uniformity for Kleene wands (Def
5.8
) and define the notion of a (strongly) inductive Kleene wand (Def
5.13
). We note that an interference restriction category can have at most one inductive Kleene wand (Lemma
5.14
.(
ii
)). Indeed, in the presence of countable disjoint joins, there is a canonical strongly inductive Kleene wand given by the expected join of iterations formula (Thm
5.16
).

In Sec
6
, we introduce classical interference restriction categories (Def
6.1
) and Kleene upper-stars (Def
6.6
). We then show that for a classical interference restriction category, there is a bijective correspondence between Kleene wands and Kleene upper-stars (Thm
6.9
).

In Sec
7
, we review extensive restriction categories and decisions. We then introduce the notion of being decision disjoint (Def
7.8
) and show that this induces an interference relation on an extensive restriction category, in other words, an extensive restriction category is an interference restriction category with binary disjoint joins (Thm
7.10
).

In Sec
8
, we review the matrix representation of extensive restriction categories and how it relates to decision disjointness. We also introduce a matrix construction on a finitely disjoint interference restriction category (Def
8.1
) and show that this matrix construction is an extensive restriction category (Prop
8.2
).

In Sec
9
, we first review the correspondence between trace operators and parametrized iterations operators for coproducts. We express the axioms of a parametrized iteration operator and a trace operator using the matrix notation of an extensive restriction category (Lemma
9.8
). This leads to the main result of the paper, which is that, for extensive restriction categories, there is a bijective correspondence between parametrized iteration operators (equivalently trace operators) and Kleene wands (Thm
9.11
). In the classical setting we extend this to including Kleene upper-stars (Prop
9.13
). We conclude by explaining how computing traces of matrices of any size in a traced extensive restriction category reduces to computing traces of
2\times 2
matrices, which allows us to show that applying the matrix construction to an itegory results in a traced extensive restriction category (Prop
9.15
).

Future Directions:

In future work, it would be interesting to study how Kleene wands connect up with other categorifications of iteration, such as iteration theories
[
7
]
, Elgot theories
[
6
]
, and Elgot monads
[
5
,
23
]
. Moreover, it would also be interesting to consider what happens to the Kleene wand when we apply the
\mathsf{INT}
-construction
[
34
, Sec 4]
or
\mathsf{GOI}
-construction
[
1
, Sec 3]
(which are isomorphic constructions
[
24
, Prop 2.3.6]
) on a traced extensive restriction category to obtain a compact closed category. This would then allow us to relate Kleene wands to the Geometry of Interaction (GOI), either via GOI situations
[
3
, Def 4.1]
or via GOI interpretations
[
26
, Def 15]
. On the other hand, Kleene wands can naturally be interpreted by the basic programming control structure:

f\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}g:={\sf until}~~\overline{g}~~{\sf do}~~f~~{\sf end};~~g

where
\overline{g}
is interpreted as the test of whether a value lies in the domain of
g
.
This suggests that the Kleene wand could be used as a primitive to interpret imperative
style programs and flow diagrams which use “looping” constructs as above. Thus, a natural
question is how itegories relate to other models of computability. In particular, it would be interesting to revisit Kleene wands in Turing categories
[
16
,
13
]
which are based on partial combinatory algebras.

Conventions:

For an arbitrary category
\mathbb{X}
, we will denote objects using capital letters
A
,
B
,
C
etc., homsets will be denoted as
\mathbb{X}(A,B)
and maps will be denoted by lowercase letters
f,g,h,etc.\in\mathbb{X}(A,B)
. Arbitrary maps will be denoted using an arrow
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
, identity maps as
1_{A}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
, and, for composition, we will use
diagrammatic
notation
(as opposed to
applicative
notation), that is, the composition of
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
followed by
g:B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}C
is denoted as
fg:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}C
(rather than
g\circ f
).

2
Restriction Categories

In this background section, we review restriction categories and the necessary basic theory for the story of this paper such as restriction idempotents, the canonical partial order on maps, and restriction zero maps, as well as some main examples. For a more in-depth introduction to restriction categories, we invite the reader to see
[
17
,
18
,
19
,
21
,
14
]
.

Definition 2.1

For a category
\mathbb{X}
, a
restriction operator

\overline{(\phantom{f})}

[
17
, Sec 2.1.1]
is a family of operators:

\overline{(\phantom{f})}:\mathbb{X}(A,B)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{X}(A,A)~~~~~~~~~\begin{matrix}[c]\overline{f}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Af:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B\end{matrix}

such that the following four axioms hold:

[R.1]

\overline{f}f=f
, for all
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
;

[R.2]

\overline{f}\overline{g}=\overline{g}\overline{f}
, for all
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
and
g:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}C
;

[R.3]

\overline{\overline{g}f}=\overline{g}\overline{f}
, for all
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
and
g:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}C
;

[R.4]

f\overline{g}=\overline{fg}f
, for all
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
and
g:B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}C
.

For a map
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
, the map
{\overline{f}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A}
is called the
restriction of
f
. A
restriction category
is a category equipped with a restriction operator. Furthermore, in a restriction category:

(i)

A
total
map
[
17
, Sec 2.1.2]
is a map
{f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B}
such that
\overline{f}=1_{A}
;

(ii)

For parallel maps
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
and
g:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
we say that
f\leq g
if
\overline{f}g=f

[
17
, Sec 2.1.4]
;

(iii)

A
restriction idempotent

[
17
, Sec 2.3.3]
is a map
e:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
such that
\overline{e}=e
. For every object
A\in\mathbb{X}
, we denote by
\mathcal{O}(A)
the set of restriction idempotents of type
A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
.

The main intuition for a restriction category is that maps
f
are partial and the restriction
\overline{f}
captures the domain of definition of
f
. As we will see in Ex
2.3
below, the standard intuition for
\overline{f}
is checking if
x\in A
is in the domain of
f
or not, that is,
\overline{f}(x)=x
when
f(x)
is defined and
\overline{f}(x)
is undefined when
f(x)
is undefined. Thus total maps are interpreted as maps which are everywhere defined.

The intuition for the partial order
\leq
is that
f\leq g
means whenever
f
is defined,
g
is also defined and is equal to
f
. Moreover, this partial order enriches every restriction category
\mathbb{X}
over posets. Thus, each homset
(\mathbb{X}(A,B),\leq)
is a poset and composition is monotone, that is if
f\leq g
then for suitable maps
h
and
k
,
hfk\leq hgk
. Also observe that
f\leq g
implies that
\overline{f}\leq\overline{g}
(though the converse is not necessarily true).

Restriction idempotents correspond to the domain of definition of maps, and thus
\mathcal{O}(A)
may be interpreted as certain special subsets of the object
A
. The name restriction idempotent is justified since a restriction idempotent
e
is indeed idempotent,
ee=e
. In particular, every restriction
\overline{f}
is a restriction idempotent, so we have
\overline{\overline{f}}=\overline{f}
and
\overline{f}~\overline{f}=\overline{f}
. Furthermore,
\mathcal{O}(A)
has a natural bounded meet-semilattice structure, where the meet is given by composition,
e_{0}\wedge e_{1}=e_{0}e_{1}
, and the top element is the identity
1_{A}
. The induced partial order on
\mathcal{O}(A)
on this lattice structure is the same as the restriction order. Thus, for restriction idempotents,
e_{0}\leq e_{1}
if and only if
e_{0}e_{1}=e_{0}
, and also
e\leq 1_{A}
.

A key notion for the story of this paper is restriction zero maps: these are intuitively maps which are nowhere defined. Categorically speaking, a restriction zero map is a zero map in the usual sense whose restriction is also a zero map. Recall a category has zero maps if for every pair of objects
A
and
B
there is a map
0:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
which satisfies the annihilation property, that is,
f0=0=0f
for all maps
f
.

Definition 2.2

A restriction category is said to have
restriction zeroes

[
19
, Sec 2.2]
if it has zero maps
{0:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B}
such that
\overline{0}=0
.

Observe that for any map
f
,
0\leq f
. Thus restriction zero maps are the bottom elements for each homset. Moreover, the restriction zero map of type
0:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is a restriction idempotent, and is, therefore, the bottom element of
\mathcal{O}(A)
.

We conclude this background section by providing two running examples of restriction categories. For a list of many other examples of restriction categories, see
[
17
, Sec 2.1.3]
.

Example 2.3

Let
\mathsf{PAR}
be the category whose objects are sets and whose maps are partial functions between sets. Then
\mathsf{PAR}
is a restriction category
[
17
, Ex 2.1.3.1]
where the restriction of a partial function
f:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
is the partial function
{\overline{f}:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X}
defined as follows:

\overline{f}(x)=\begin{cases}x&\text{if }f(x)\downarrow\\
\uparrow&\text{if }f(x)\uparrow\end{cases}

where
\downarrow
means defined and
\uparrow
means undefined. The total maps in
\mathsf{PAR}
are precisely the functions that are everywhere defined – thus the subcategory of total maps of
\mathsf{PAR}
is simply the category of sets and functions. For partial functions
f:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
and
g:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
, we have that
f\leq g
if
g(x)=f(x)
whenever
f(x)\downarrow
. The restriction idempotents for a set
X
are in bijective correspondence to subsets of
X

[
15
, Ex 2.2.1]
. In particular, for every subset
U\subseteq X
, define the partial function
e_{U}:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X
as follows:

\displaystyle e_{U}(x)\colon=\begin{cases}x&\text{ if }x\in U\\
\uparrow&\text{ if }x\notin U\end{cases}

Then clearly
\overline{e_{U}}=e_{U}
, so
e_{U}
is a restriction idempotent, and every restriction idempotent is of this form. In particular, note that
\overline{f}=e_{\mathsf{dom}(f)}
. So
\mathcal{O}(X)
is isomorphic to the powerset
\mathcal{P}(X)
of
X
,
\mathcal{O}(X)\cong\mathcal{P}(X)
. Lastly,
\mathsf{PAR}
has restriction zero maps where
0:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
is the partial function which is nowhere defined, i.e.,
0(x)\uparrow
for all
x\in X
.

Example 2.4

Let
\mathbb{N}
be the set of natural numbers, and let
\mathsf{REC}
be the category whose objects are finite powers
\mathbb{N}^{n}
and where a map
f:\mathbb{N}^{n}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{N}^{m}
is a partial function which is an
m
-tuple
f=\langle f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}\rangle
of partial recursive functions in
n
variables
f_{i}:\mathbb{N}^{n}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{N}
. Then
\mathsf{REC}
is a restriction category
[
14
, Ex 2.1.(ii)]
with the same restriction operator as
\mathsf{PAR}
in the previous example, where note that if
f
is a tuple of partial recursive functions, then
\overline{f}
will also be a tuple of partial recursive functions. In other words,
\mathsf{REC}
is a sub-restriction category of
\mathsf{PAR}
. The total maps in
\mathsf{REC}
correspond to tuples of total recursive functions. The partial order
\leq
is again the same as
\mathsf{PAR}
. On the other hand, the restriction idempotents for
\mathbb{N}^{n}
in
\mathsf{REC}
correspond to the recursive enumerable subsets of
\mathbb{N}^{n}

[
15
, Ex 2.2.3]
. As such, if
U\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{n}
is recursively enumerable, then
e_{U}:\mathbb{N}^{n}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{N}^{n}
(as defined in the previous example) will be a map in
\mathsf{REC}
, and thus a restriction idempotent in
\mathsf{REC}
. Lastly,
\mathsf{REC}
also has restriction zero maps defined in the same way as in
\mathsf{PAR}
since the nowhere defined partial function is recursive. It is also worth mentioning that
\mathsf{REC}
is the canonical example of a
Turing category

[
16
, Ex 3.2.1]
, which briefly is a restriction category with an internal partial combinatory algebra satisfying a universal property which encodes computability of maps.

3
Interference

In this section we introduce the concept of an interference relation, which is a relation on maps of restriction category with restriction zeroes which tells us when two maps that have the same domain should be viewed as being formally disjoint. Formal disjointness must certainly imply that where one map is defined, the other is undefined, but could possibly also ask for more. This concept of disjointness plays key a role in the definition of an iteration operator, since in particular it is used to determine the termination condition of iterations.

Definition 3.1

For a restriction category
\mathbb{X}
with restriction zeroes, an
interference relation

\perp
on maps is a family of relations (indexed by triples of objects
A,B,C\in\mathbb{X}
) on homsets
\perp^{A}_{B,C}\subseteq\mathbb{X}(A,B)\times\mathbb{X}(A,C)
, where we write
f\perp g
when
(f,g)\in\perp^{A}_{B,C}
and say that
f
and
g
are
\perp
-disjoint
, and such that the following axioms hold:

[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.0]

Zero:
1_{A}\perp 0
;

[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.1]

Symmetry: If
f\perp g
, then
g\perp f
;

[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.2]

Anti-reflexive: If
f\perp f
, then
f=0
;

[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.3]

Downward closed: If
f^{\prime}\perp g^{\prime}
,
f\leq f^{\prime}
, and
g\leq g^{\prime}
, then
f\perp g
;

[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.4]

Composition: If
f\perp g
, then for all suitable maps
h
,
k
, and
k^{\prime}
, we have that
hfk\perp hgk^{\prime}
;

[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.5]

Restriction: If
f\perp g
, then
\overline{f}\perp\overline{g}
.

It turns out that for an interference relation
\perp
, maps are
\perp
-disjoint if and only if their restrictions are
\perp
-disjoint. As such this implies that an interference relation induces a
restrictional coherence

[
21
, Def 6.1]
on parallel maps.

Lemma 3.2

For an interference relation
\perp
,
f\perp g
if and only if
\overline{f}\perp\overline{g}
.

Proof:
The
\Rightarrow
direction is simply
[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.5]
. For the
\Leftarrow
direction, suppose that
\overline{f}\perp\overline{g}
. Applying
[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.4]
, we get that
\overline{f}f\perp\overline{g}g
, which by
[R.1]
gives us that
f\perp g
, as desired.

\Box

Thus, an interference relation is completely determined by its behaviour on restriction idempotents. This means that an interference relation can equivalently be defined as a family of relations on restriction idempotents.

Definition 3.3

For a restriction category
\mathbb{X}
with restriction zero maps, a
restrictional interference relation

\perp
is a family of relations (indexed by the objects of
\mathbb{X}
) between restriction idempotents
\perp_{A}^{\mathcal{O}}\subseteq\mathcal{O}(A)\times\mathcal{O}(A)
, where again we write
e\perp e^{\prime}
if
(e,e^{\prime})\in\perp_{A}^{\mathcal{O}}
, and such that the following axioms hold:

[
\mathcal{O}\!\boldsymbol{\perp}
.0]

Zero:
1_{A}\perp 0
;

[
\mathcal{O}\!\boldsymbol{\perp}
.1]

Symmetry: If
e\perp e^{\prime}
, then
e^{\prime}\perp e
;

[
\mathcal{O}\!\boldsymbol{\perp}
.2]

Anti-Reflexive: If
e\perp e
, then
e=0
;

[
\mathcal{O}\!\boldsymbol{\perp}
.3]

Downward Closed: If
e^{\prime}_{1}\perp e^{\prime}_{2}
,
e_{1}\leq e^{\prime}_{1}
, and
e_{2}\leq e_{2}^{\prime}
, then
e_{1}\perp e_{2}
;

[
\mathcal{O}\!\boldsymbol{\perp}
.4]

Pre-Composition: If
e\perp e^{\prime}
in
{\cal O}(A)
, then for any
h
with codomain
A
,
\overline{he}\perp\overline{he^{\prime}}
.

Proposition 3.4

For a restriction category with restriction zero maps, there is a bijective correspondence between interference relations on maps and restrictional interference relations. Explicitly,

(i)

Given an interference relation
\perp
, define the restrictional interference relation
\perp^{\mathcal{O}}
as
e\perp^{\mathcal{O}}e^{\prime}
if and only if
e\perp e^{\prime}
;

(ii)

Given a restrictional interference relation
\perp^{\mathcal{O}}
, define the interference relation
\perp
as
f\perp g
if and only if
\overline{f}\perp^{\mathcal{O}}\overline{g}
.

Proof:
Starting with an interference relation
\perp
, clearly by definition
\perp^{\mathcal{O}}
satisfies
[
\mathcal{O}\!\boldsymbol{\perp}
.0]
to
[
\mathcal{O}\!\boldsymbol{\perp}
.3]
, since they are the same as
[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.0]
to
[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.3]
. It remains to explain why
[
\mathcal{O}\!\boldsymbol{\perp}
.4]
holds. So suppose that
e\perp^{\mathcal{O}}e^{\prime}
, meaning that
e\perp e^{\prime}
. Then by
[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.4]
, we have that
he\perp he^{\prime}
, and then by
[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.5]
that
\overline{he}\perp\overline{he^{\prime}}
, thus
\overline{he}\perp^{\mathcal{O}}\overline{he^{\prime}}
. So we conclude that
\perp^{\mathcal{O}}
is indeed a restrictional interference relation.

Conversely, starting with a restrictional interference relation
\perp^{\mathcal{O}}
, we need to show that
\perp
satisfies the six axioms
[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.0]
to
[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.5]
.

[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.0]

By
[
\mathcal{O}\!\boldsymbol{\perp}
.0]
,
1_{A}\perp^{\mathcal{O}}0
. However, since
1_{A}
and
0
are both restriction idempotents, we also have that
\overline{1_{A}}\perp^{\mathcal{O}}\overline{0}
, which tells us that

1_{A}\perp 0
.

[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.1]

Suppose that
f\perp g
, meaning
\overline{f}\perp^{\mathcal{O}}\overline{g}
. By
[
\mathcal{O}\!\boldsymbol{\perp}
.1]
, this means that
\overline{g}\perp^{\mathcal{O}}\overline{f}
, so we have that

g\perp f
.

[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.2]

Suppose that
f\perp f
, that is
\overline{f}\perp^{\mathcal{O}}\overline{f}
. By
[
\mathcal{O}\!\boldsymbol{\perp}
.2]
, this means that
\overline{f}=0
. However, by
[R.1]
,
\overline{f}=0
implies that
f=0
.

[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.3]

Suppose that
f^{\prime}\perp g^{\prime}
, meaning
\overline{f^{\prime}}\perp^{\mathcal{O}}\overline{g^{\prime}}
, and that
f\leq f^{\prime}
and
g\leq g^{\prime}
. This implies that
\overline{f}\leq\overline{f^{\prime}}
and
\overline{g}\leq\overline{g^{\prime}}
as well. So by
[
\mathcal{O}\!\boldsymbol{\perp}
.3]
, we have that
\overline{f}\perp^{\mathcal{O}}\overline{g}
, and
so
f\perp g
.

[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.4]

First recall that in a restriction category, we have the identity
\overline{fg}=\overline{f\overline{g}}

[
17
, Lemma 2.1.(iii)]
. Now suppose that
f\perp g
, meaning
\overline{f}\perp^{\mathcal{O}}\overline{g}
. Then for any suitable
h
, by
[
\mathcal{O}\!\boldsymbol{\perp}
.4]
we have that
\overline{h\overline{f}}\perp^{\mathcal{O}}\overline{h\overline{g}}
. However, by the previous mentioned identity, we may rewrite this as
\overline{hf}\perp^{\mathcal{O}}\overline{hg}
. Now observe that for any suitable
k
and
k^{\prime}
,
\overline{hfk}\leq\overline{hf}
and
\overline{hgk^{\prime}}\leq\overline{hg}
. Then by
[
\mathcal{O}\!\boldsymbol{\perp}
.3]
, we have that
\overline{hfk}\perp^{\mathcal{O}}\overline{hgk^{\prime}}
. As such, we have that
hfk\perp hgk^{\prime}
.

[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.5]

Suppose that
f\perp g
, meaning
\overline{f}\perp^{\mathcal{O}}\overline{g}
. However, since
\overline{f}
and
\overline{g}
are restriction idempotents, we can rewrite this as
\overline{\overline{f}}\perp^{\mathcal{O}}\overline{\overline{g}}
, meaning that
\overline{f}\perp\overline{g}
.

So
\perp
is indeed an interference relation on maps.

It remains to explain why these constructions are inverses of each other. Clearly, starting with a restrictional interference relation
\perp^{\mathcal{O}}
, building its associated interference relation on maps,

\perp=\{(f,g)|~\overline{f}\perp^{\mathcal{O}}\overline{g}\}
and then taking the induced restrictional interference relation,
\{(\overline{f},\overline{g})|~f\perp g\}
gives us back the starting restrictional interference relation
\perp^{\mathcal{O}}
. Conversely, starting with an interference relation on maps
\perp
, building its associated restrictional interference relation
\perp^{\mathcal{O}}
, and then taking the induced interference relation on maps results back to
\perp
by Lemma
3.2
.

\Box

From now on, we will make no distinction between an interference relation and its corresponding restrictional interference relation and shall denote them both by
\perp
. Here are some other basic yet important properties of interference relations:

Lemma 3.5

Let
\perp
be an interference relation on a restriction category
\mathbb{X}
with restriction zeroes. Then:

(i)

For all maps
f
,
f\perp 0
;

(ii)

If
f\perp g
then
\overline{f}g=0
,
\overline{g}f=0
, and
\overline{f}~\overline{g}=0
;

(iii)

If
f
is total, then
f\perp g
if and only if
g=0
.

(iv)

If
e
is a restriction idempotent, then
e\perp f
if and only if
e\perp\overline{f}
.

(v)

If
f\perp g
, then
f\perp\overline{g}
and
\overline{f}\perp g
.

In particular notice that Lemma
3.5
.(
ii
) says that if maps are
\perp
-disjoint, then they are disjoint in the usual restriction category sense
[
21
, Prop 6.2]
.

Proof:
These are mostly straightforward.

(i)

By
[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.0]
,
1_{A}\perp 0
. Using
[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.4]
, pre-composing both sides by
f
, we get that
f\perp 0
.

(ii)

First note that
\overline{f}g=0
,
\overline{g}f=0
, and
\overline{f}~\overline{g}=0
are all equivalent conditions
[
21
, Prop 6.2]
. So suppose that
f\perp g
. We will show that
\overline{f}\overline{g}=0
from the anti-reflexivity of
\perp
. So firstly if
f\perp g
, then by
[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.5]
,
\overline{f}\perp\overline{g}
. Now note that
\overline{f}~\overline{g~}\overline{f}=\overline{f}~\overline{g}
and
\overline{f}~\overline{g}~\overline{g}=\overline{f}~\overline{g}
. So by
[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.4]
, pre-composing both sides by
\overline{f}~\overline{g}
we get that
\overline{f}~\overline{g}\perp\overline{f}~\overline{g}
. Then
[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.2]
implies that
\overline{f}~\overline{g}=0
.

(iii)

The
\Leftarrow
direction is simply
[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.0]
. So for the
\Rightarrow
direction, suppose that
f
is total and that
f\perp g
. Then by (
ii
), we have that
\overline{f}g=0
. However since
f
is total,
\overline{f}=1
. So we have that
g=0
.

(iv)

This follows immediately from Lemma
3.2
.

(v)

This follows from (
iv
) and
[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.5]
.

\Box

A restriction category with restriction zeroes can have multiple different interference relations. In fact, there are always at least two possible ones: a minimal one
\perp_{\delta}
and a maximal one
\perp_{0}
(with respect to the usual ordering of relations). The minimal interference relation
\perp_{\delta}
is the relation which only relates maps to zero, that is
f\perp_{\delta}g
if and only if
f=0
or
g=0
. On the other hand, the maximal interference relation
\perp_{0}
is the relation which says that two maps are disjoint if, whenever one is defined, the other is not. Thus,
f\perp_{0}g
if and only if
\overline{f}g=0
(equivalently
\overline{g}f=0
) or, in other words, they are disjoint in the sense of
[
21
, Prop 6.2]
.

Lemma 3.6

For a restriction category
\mathbb{X}
with restriction zero maps,

(i)

\perp_{0}
is an interference relation with its associated restrictional interference relation given by
e\perp_{0}e^{\prime}
if and only if
ee^{\prime}=0
;

(ii)

\perp_{\delta}
is an interference relation with its associated restrictional interference relation given by
e\perp_{\delta}e^{\prime}
if and only if
e=0
or
e^{\prime}=0
.

Furthermore, for every interference relation
\perp
on
\mathbb{X}
, we have that
\perp_{\delta}\subseteq\perp\subseteq\perp_{0}
.

Proof:
Checking that
\perp_{\delta}
is an interference relation is straightforward and is left as an exercise for the reader. We now check that
\perp_{0}
is also an interference relation by proving it is a restrictional interference relation.

[
\mathcal{O}\!\boldsymbol{\perp}
.0]

By the annihilation property we have that
1_{A}0=0
, so
1_{A}\perp_{0}0
.

[
\mathcal{O}\!\boldsymbol{\perp}
.1]

Recall that restriction idempotents commute, that is,
ee^{\prime}=e^{\prime}e
. Therefore, if
e\perp_{0}e^{\prime}
, which means that
ee^{\prime}=0
, then
e^{\prime}e=0
as well, and so
e^{\prime}\perp_{0}e
.

[
\mathcal{O}\!\boldsymbol{\perp}
.2]

Recall that restriction idempotents are idempotents, that is, if
e\in\mathcal{O}(A)
, then
ee=e
. Then if
e\perp_{0}e
, which implies that
ee=0
, then we get that
e=0
.

[
\mathcal{O}\!\boldsymbol{\perp}
.3]

Recall that for restriction idempotents,
e_{1}\leq e^{\prime}_{1}
and
e_{2}\leq e_{2}^{\prime}
imply that
e_{1}e^{\prime}_{1}=e_{1}
and
e^{\prime}_{2}e_{2}=e_{2}
. Then if
e^{\prime}_{1}\perp_{0}e^{\prime}_{2}
, which means
e^{\prime}_{1}e^{\prime}_{2}=0
, then we also get that
e_{1}e_{2}=e_{1}e^{\prime}_{1}e^{\prime}_{2}e_{2}=e_{1}0e_{2}=0
. So
e_{1}e_{2}=0
and so
e_{1}\perp_{0}e_{2}
.

[
\mathcal{O}\!\boldsymbol{\perp}
.4]

First note that using
[R.3]
and
[R.4]
, it follows that
\overline{he}~\overline{he^{\prime}}=\overline{hee^{\prime}}
. So if
e\perp_{0}e^{\prime}
, which means
ee^{\prime}=0
, then we get that
\overline{he}\overline{he^{\prime}}=\overline{hee^{\prime}}=\overline{h0}=\overline{0}=0
. So have that
\overline{he}\perp_{0}\overline{he^{\prime}}
.

So
\perp_{0}
is a restrictional interference relation, and it is straightforward to see that its induced interference relation is precisely as defined above. So we conclude that
\perp_{0}
is an interference relation.

It remains to explain why
\perp_{0}
is maximal and
\perp_{\delta}
is minimal amongst interference relations. However we have already done this, since for any interference relation
\perp
, that
\perp\subseteq\perp_{0}
is precisely the statement of Lemma
3.5
.(
ii
), while that
\perp_{\delta}\subseteq\perp
is precisely Lemma
3.5
.(
i
).

\Box

Thus, having an interference relation is chosen structure rather than a property and we say that:

Definition 3.7

An
interference restriction category
is pair
(\mathbb{X},\perp)
consisting of a restriction category
\mathbb{X}
with restriction zeroes and a chosen a interference relation
\perp
.

Nevertheless, to avoid heavy nomenclature, we will say that
\mathbb{X}
is a
\perp
-restriction category as a short hand for saying that
(\mathbb{X},\perp)
is an interference restriction category. We conclude this section with our main examples of interference relations:

Example 3.8

In
\mathsf{PAR}
, the maximal restrictional interference relation corresponds to checking when subsets are disjoint. Indeed, for subsets
U,V\subseteq X
, for their associated restriction idempotents we have that
e_{U}\perp_{0}e_{V}
if and only if
U\cap V=\emptyset
. Therefore, the corresponding maximal interference relation is given as follows: for partial functions
{f:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y}
and
g:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Z
, we have that
f\perp_{0}g
if and only if their domains are disjoint,
\mathsf{dom}(f)\cap\mathsf{dom}(g)=\emptyset
. In other words,
f\perp_{0}g
if
f(x)\uparrow
whenever
g(x)\downarrow
and
g(x)\uparrow
whenever
f(x)\downarrow
. On the other hand, the minimal restrictional interference relation corresponds to checking if either subset is empty. So for
U,V\subseteq X
, for their associated restriction idempotents we have that
e_{U}\perp_{\delta}e_{V}
if and only if
U=\emptyset
or
V=\emptyset
. As such, for partial functions
{f:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y}
and
{g:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Z}
, we have that
f\perp_{\delta}g
if and only if either
f
or
g
is nowhere define, that is, if
\mathsf{dom}(f)=\emptyset
or
\mathsf{dom}(g)=\emptyset
.

Example 3.9

Here is an example of an interference relation on
\mathsf{REC}
which is neither the maximal one
\perp_{0}
nor the minimal one
\perp_{\delta}
. Recall that a subset
U\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{n}
is recursive (or computable) if the characteristic function
\chi_{U}:\mathbb{N}^{n}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{N}
defined as
\chi_{U}(x)=1
if
x\in U
and
\chi_{X}(x)=0
if
x\notin U
is a total recursive function. Then two subsets
U,V\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{n}
are said to be
recursively separable
if there exists a recursive set
W\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{n}
such that
U\subseteq W
and
V\cap W=\emptyset
. Then define the restrictional interference relation
\perp_{r}
for the associated restriction idempotents of recursively enumerable subsets
U,V\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{n}
as
e_{U}\perp_{r}e_{V}
if and only if
U
and
V
are recursively separable. Then the induced interference relation
\perp_{r}
is given as follows: for two maps
F:\mathbb{N}^{n}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{N}^{m}
and
G:\mathbb{N}^{n}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{N}^{k}
in
\mathsf{REC}
, we have that
F\perp_{r}G
if their domains
\mathsf{dom}(F)
and
\mathsf{dom}(G)
are recursively separable. Then
\perp_{r}
is an interference coherence on
\mathsf{REC}
. On the one hand, clearly
\perp_{r}
is not the minimal one
\perp_{\delta}
. On the other hand, note that
\perp_{0}
on
\mathsf{REC}
is given the same way as in
\mathsf{PAR}
, which means that
\perp_{r}
and
\perp_{0}
are indeed different since there exists disjoint recursively enumerable sets which are recursively inseparable (i.e. not recursively separable).

4
Disjoint Joins

In this section we discuss the notion of
disjoint joins
in an interference restriction category. Since the homsets of a restriction category are posets, we can consider the
join

[
21
, Def 6.7]
of suitable parallel maps to be the join in usual sense, meaning the least upper bound with respect to
\leq
. However, for the story of this paper, we do not need all joins to exists like in a join restriction category
[
21
, Def 10.1]
, but instead only require the joins of
\perp
-disjoint maps. As such, we call such joins:
\perp
-disjoint
joins, or simply
\perp
-joins, and denote them using
\sqcup
instead of
\vee
, to further emphasize that the maps we are working with are intuitively disjoint. Readers familiar with joins in a restriction category may recall that joins can only be defined for maps that are
compatible

[
21
, Prop 6.3]
. Recall that parallel maps
f
and
g
are compatible, written as
f\smile g
, if
\overline{f}g=\overline{g}f
. However, by Lemma
3.5
.(
ii
), if
f\perp g
, then
\overline{g}f=0=\overline{f}g
, and so
f\smile g
. Therefore, we can indeed consider the join of parallel
\perp
-disjoint maps. More generally, one can define define joins with respect to a restrictional coherence
[
21
, Def 6.7]
. Thus, since every interference relation is a restrictional coherence, the following definition is
[
21
, Def 6.7]
for the special case of an interference relation.

Definition 4.1

In a
\perp
-restriction category
\mathbb{X}
, the
\perp
-join
(if it exists) of a family of pairwise
\perp
-disjoint parallel maps
{\{f_{i}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B\}_{i\in I}}\subseteq\mathbb{X}(A,B)
(where
I
is an arbitrary index set and
f_{i}\perp f_{j}
for all
i,j\in I
with
i\neq j
) is a (necessarily unique) map
{\bigsqcup\limits_{i\in I}f_{i}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B}
such that:

[
\boldsymbol{\sqcup}
.1]

f_{i}\leq\bigsqcup\limits_{i\in I}f_{i}
for all
i\in I
;

[
\boldsymbol{\sqcup}
.2]

If
g:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
is a map such that
f_{i}\leq g
for all
i\in I
, then
\bigsqcup\limits_{i\in I}f_{i}\leq g
.

Note that
\perp
-joins are necessarily unique since
[
\boldsymbol{\sqcup}
.1]
and
[
\boldsymbol{\sqcup}
.2]
say that they are least upper-bounds, which are always unique. For a finite family indexed by
I=\{0,\ldots,n+1\}
, we write their
\perp
-join as
\bigsqcup\limits_{i\in I}f_{i}=f_{0}\sqcup...\sqcup f_{n+1}
. In the special case of a singleton
\{f\}
, its
\perp
-join is just
f
, while for the case of the empty set, the
\perp
-join of nothing is simply the zero map. When the
\perp
-joins of all suitable families of
\perp
-disjoint maps exists, we may also ask that they be compatible with composition. In
[
21
, Def 6.7]
, preservations of joins by pre-composition was called being
stable
, while preservations of joins by post-composition was called being
universal
. It turns out that being stable implies being universal
[
21
, Lemma 6.10.(2)]
, so we only need to ask that pre-composition preserves
\perp
-joins. Since for most of the story of this paper, we only really need
binary

\perp
-joins, we take the time to define this case explicitly.

Definition 4.2

A
\perp
-restriction category
\mathbb{X}
is said to have
all binary
\perp
-joins
if for every pair of
\perp
-disjoint parallel maps
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
and
g:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
(i.e.
f\perp g
) their
\perp
-join
f\sqcup g:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
exists and:

[
\boldsymbol{\sqcup}
.3]

For any map
h:A^{\prime}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
,
h\left(f\sqcup g\right)=hf\sqcup hg
.

Similarly, a
\perp
-restriction category
\mathbb{X}
is said to have
all (finite)
\perp
-joins
if for every (finite) family of pairwise
\perp
-disjoint parallel maps
{\{f_{i}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B\}_{i\in I}}
, their
\perp
-join
{\bigsqcup\limits_{i\in I}f_{i}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B}
exists and:

[
\boldsymbol{\sqcup}
.3]

For any map
h:A^{\prime}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
,
h\bigsqcup\limits_{i\in I}f_{i}=\bigsqcup\limits_{i\in I}hf_{i}
.

Since a
\perp
-join is a join in the sense of
[
21
, Def 6.7]
, it therefore satisfies the same properties, some of which can be found in
[
12
, Prop 2.14]
and
[
20
, Lemma 4.3]
. In the result below, which highlights some useful identities,
I
is an arbitrary indexing set: if
I
has size two we recover the binary case, if
I
is finite we recover the finite case.

Lemma 4.3

Let
\mathbb{X}
be a
\perp
-restriction category with all (binary/finite)
\perp
-joins, then:

(i)

\overline{f_{j}}\bigsqcup\limits_{i\in I}f_{i}=f_{j}
for all
j\in I
;

(ii)

\overline{\bigsqcup\limits_{i\in I}f_{i}}=\bigsqcup\limits_{i\in I}\overline{f_{i}}
;

(iii)

k\left(\bigsqcup\limits_{i\in I}f_{i}\right)k^{\prime}=\bigsqcup\limits_{i\in I}kf_{i}k^{\prime}
;

(iv)

If
\bigsqcup\limits_{i\in I}f_{i}\perp h
then
f_{j}\perp h
for all
j\in I
.

Proof:
The first three are simply
[
12
, Prop 2.14.(i)-(iii)]
for the special case of our
\perp
-joins, and are proven using the same calculations. For (
iv
), suppose that
\bigsqcup\limits_{i\in I}f_{i}\perp h
. By
[
\boldsymbol{\sqcup}
.1]
,
f_{j}\leq\bigsqcup\limits_{i\in I}f_{i}
, and so by
[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.3]
, we get that
f_{j}\perp h
.

\Box

It is important to note that while having finite
\perp
-joins obviously implies having binary
\perp
-joins, the converse is not necessarily true. Indeed, having binary
\perp
-joins does not imply we can build arbitrary finite
\perp
-joins since the converse of Lemma
4.3
.(
iv
) does not hold in general. To see this, suppose we have three parallel maps such that
f\perp g
,
g\perp h
, and
f\perp h
. Then we get their binary
\perp
-joins
f\sqcup g
,
g\sqcup h
, and
f\sqcup h
. However since we do not necessarily have that say
f\sqcup g
and
h
are
\perp
-disjoint, we wouldn’t be able to construct
\left(f\sqcup g\right)\sqcup h
. Thus, we also need to ask that
\perp
-joins preserve
\perp
-disjointness.

Definition 4.4

In a
\perp
-restriction category
\mathbb{X}
, if the
\perp
-join
{\bigsqcup\limits_{i\in I}f_{i}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B}
of a family of pairwise
\perp
-disjoint parallel maps
{\{f_{i}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B\}_{i\in I}}
exists, then we say that
\bigsqcup\limits_{i\in I}f_{i}
is
strong
if:

[
\boldsymbol{\sqcup}
.4]

For every map
h:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}C
such that
f_{i}\perp h
for all
i\in I
, then
\bigsqcup\limits_{i\in I}f_{i}\perp h
.

A
(finitely) disjoint
\perp
-restriction category
is a
\perp
-restriction category
\mathbb{X}
that has all (finite)
\perp
-joins and every (finite)
\perp
-join is strong.

We can now show that having strong binary
\perp
-joins is equivalent to having strong finite
\perp
-joins.

Lemma 4.5

Let
\mathbb{X}
be a
\perp
-restriction category. Then
\mathbb{X}
is a finitely disjoint
\perp
-restriction category if and only if
\mathbb{X}
has all binary
\perp
-joins and every binary
\perp
-join is strong.

Proof:
The
\Leftarrow
direction is automatic. For the
\Rightarrow
direction, suppose we have all binary
\perp
-joins and that they are strong. As mentioned above, the
\perp
-join of the empty family is the zero map, the
\perp
-join of a singleton is the map itself, and by assumption we also have binary
\perp
-joins. So we need to show that for any finite family of pairwise
\perp
-disjoint parallel maps of size greater than two, their
\perp
-join exists, is preserved by pre-composition, and is strong. We do so by induction, where the base case is a family of size
2
, which we already know holds. So suppose that we can build
n
-ary
\perp
-joins which are preserved by pre-composition
[
\boldsymbol{\sqcup}
.3]
and strong
[
\boldsymbol{\sqcup}
.4]
. Let
\{f_{0},f_{1},\ldots,f_{n},f_{n+1}\}
be a family of pairwise
\perp
-disjoint parallel maps. Note that
\{f_{0},f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}\}
is also a family of pairwise
\perp
-disjoint parallel maps, so by the induction hypothesis, we get its
\perp
-join
f_{0}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup f_{n}
. By
[
\boldsymbol{\sqcup}
.4]
, we get that
f_{0}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup f_{n}\perp f_{n+1}
, so we can define
f_{0}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup f_{n}\sqcup f_{n+1}
as the binary join
f_{0}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup f_{n}\sqcup f_{n+1}:=\left(f_{0}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup f_{n}\right)\sqcup f_{n+1}
. It is straightforward to check that
f_{0}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup f_{n}\sqcup f_{n+1}
satisfies
[
\boldsymbol{\sqcup}
.1]
and
[
\boldsymbol{\sqcup}
.2]
, so
f_{0}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup f_{n}\sqcup f_{n+1}
is indeed the
\perp
-join of
\{f_{0},f_{1},\ldots,f_{n},f_{n+1}\}
. Now for any suitable map
h
, by
[
\boldsymbol{\sqcup}
.3]
for the binary case and the induction hypothesis, we can also easily check that
h\left(f_{0}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup f_{n}\sqcup f_{n+1}\right)=hf_{0}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup hf_{n}\sqcup hf_{n+1}
as desired. Moreover, if
k
is a map such that
k\perp f_{i}
, then by the induction hypothesis for
[
\boldsymbol{\sqcup}
.4]
, we get that
k\perp f_{0}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup f_{n}
. Then since
k\perp f_{n+1}
as well, by
[
\boldsymbol{\sqcup}
.4]
for the binary case, we get that
k\perp f_{0}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup f_{n}\sqcup f_{n+1}
. So we conclude that
\mathbb{X}
is a finitely disjoint
\perp
-restriction category.

\Box

Before finally giving examples, let us quickly take a look at disjoint joins for the maximal and minimal interference relations. We first show that for the maximal relation
\perp_{0}
, every
\perp_{0}
-join is strong. In particular this means that if we have binary
\perp_{0}
-joins, then we have all finite
\perp_{0}
-joins as well. A finitely disjoint
\perp_{0}
-restriction category was called an
\perp
-restriction category
in
[
21
, Def 7.5]
(not be confused with our use of
\perp
-restriction category for an interference restriction category which may not have
\perp
-joins).

Lemma 4.6

Let
\mathbb{X}
be a restriction category with restriction zeroes. Then any
\perp_{0}
-join which exists is strong. Therefore,
\mathbb{X}
is a finitely disjoint
\perp_{0}
-restriction category if and only if
\mathbb{X}
has all binary
\perp_{0}
-joins.

Proof:
Let
\{f_{i}\}
be a family of pairwise
\perp_{0}
-disjoint parallel maps whose
\perp_{0}
-join
\bigsqcup\limits_{i\in I}f_{i}
exists. Now suppose we also have another map
h
such that
h\perp_{0}f_{i}
for all
i
, which recall is equivalent to saying that
\overline{h}f_{i}=0
. Then by
[
\boldsymbol{\sqcup}
.3]
, we get that
\overline{h}\bigsqcup\limits_{i\in I}f_{i}=\bigsqcup\limits_{i\in I}\overline{h}f_{i}=\bigsqcup\limits_{i\in I}0=0
. So
h\perp\bigsqcup\limits_{i\in I}f_{i}
. So we get that every
\perp_{0}
-join is strong. Therefore, by Lemma
4.5
, having binary
\perp_{0}
-joins is sufficient to get all finite
\perp_{0}
-joins.

\Box

On the other hand, for the minimal interference relation
\perp_{\delta}
, all
\perp_{\delta}
-joins exist trivially and are trivially strong.

Lemma 4.7

Let
\mathbb{X}
be a restriction category with restriction zeroes. Then
\mathbb{X}
is a disjoint
\perp_{\delta}
-restriction category.

Proof:
We will first explain why every family of pairwise
\perp_{\delta}
-disjoint parallel maps can have at most two elements. Let
{\{f_{i}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B\}_{i\in I}}
be a family of pairwise
\perp_{\delta}
-disjoint parallel maps. Since
f_{i}\perp_{\delta}f_{j}
for all
i\neq j
, this implies that there is at most one non-zero map
f\in\{f_{i}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B\}_{i\in I}
and the rest must be zero. When
|I|\geq 1
, if such a non-zero
f
exists, our family is reduced to
\{f,0\}
, and if no non-zero map exists, then our family is reduced to
\{0\}
. Thus the only possible such families is the empty set
\emptyset
, singletons of every map
\{f\}
, and a two element set
\{f,0\}
where here
f\neq 0
. Clearly, the
\perp_{\delta}
-join of
\emptyset
is
0
; of
\{f\}
is
f
; and of
\{f,0\}
is
f
. So we conclude that all
\perp_{\delta}
-joins exists. It is straightforward to check that
[
\boldsymbol{\sqcup}
.3]
and
[
\boldsymbol{\sqcup}
.4]
also hold.

\Box

Here are now the disjoint joins in our main examples.

Example 4.8

\mathsf{PAR}
has all
\perp_{0}
-joins. For an
i\in I
indexed family of parallel partial functions
{f_{i}:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y}
which are pairwise
\perp_{0}
-disjoint, which recall means that when
f_{i}(x)\downarrow
then
f_{j}(x)\uparrow
for all
j\neq i
, their
\perp_{0}
-join is the partial function
\bigsqcup\limits_{i\in I}f_{i}:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
defined as follows:

\left(\bigsqcup\limits_{i\in I}f_{i}\right)(x)=\begin{cases}f_{i}(x)&\text{if there exists a (necessarily unique) $i\in I$ such that }f_{i}(x)\downarrow\\
\uparrow&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

In particular, this means that
\mathsf{PAR}
is a disjoint
\perp_{0}
-restriction category.

Example 4.9

\mathsf{REC}
has all
\perp_{r}
-joins, which are defined as in the previous example. So
\mathsf{REC}
is a disjoint
\perp_{r}
-restriction category.

We conclude this section by explaining how every interference restriction category embeds into a disjoint interference restriction category. This is similar to the join construction given in
[
21
, Sec 10]
, but which is simplified since we only want disjoint joins and not all joins. So let
\mathbb{X}
be a
\perp
-restriction category. For a family of pairwise
\perp
-disjoint parallel maps
F\subseteq\mathbb{X}(A,B)
, let
\downarrow\!\!F=\{g:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B|~\exists f\in F\text{ s.t. }g\leq f\}
. Note that
\downarrow\!\!F
need not be itself a family of pairwise
\perp
-disjoint parallel maps, and also that it is never empty since we always have that
0\in~\downarrow\!\!F
. Then, abusing notation slightly, define the disjoint
\perp
-restriction category
\mathsf{DJ}\left[(\mathbb{X},\perp)\right]
as follows:

(i)

The objects of
\mathsf{DJ}\left[(\mathbb{X},\perp)\right]
are the same as
\mathbb{X}
;

(ii)

A map
A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
in
\mathsf{DJ}\left[(\mathbb{X},\perp)\right]
is a
S=\downarrow\!\!F
where
F\subseteq\mathbb{X}(A,B)
is a family of pairwise
\perp
-disjoint parallel maps;

(iii)

Identity maps are the set of restriction idempotents
\mathcal{O}(A):A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
;

(iv)

Composition of
S:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
and
T:B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}C
is defined as
ST=\{fg|~f\in S,g\in T\}
;

(v)

The restriction of
S:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
is defined as the set of the restrictions,
\overline{S}=\{\overline{f}|~f\in S\}
;

(vi)

The restriction zero maps are the singletons
\{0\}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
;

(vii)

The interference relation is defined as
S\perp T
if for all
f\in S
and
g\in T
,
f\perp g
;

(viii)

For a family index by a set
I
of pairwise
\perp
-disjoint parallel maps
S_{i}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
, their
\perp
-join is defined as their union
\bigsqcup\limits_{i\in I}S_{i}:=\bigcup\limits_{i\in I}S_{i}
.

Now since we want to give an embedding, we also need to explain what we mean by a functor between interference restriction categories. So if
\mathbb{X}
and
\mathbb{Y}
are restriction categories, then a
restriction functor

[
17
, Sec 2.2.1]
is a functor
{\mathcal{F}:\mathbb{X}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{Y}}
which preserves the restrictions,
\overline{\mathcal{F}(f)}=\mathcal{F}\left(\overline{f}\right)
. If
(\mathbb{X},\perp)
and
(\mathbb{Y},\perp)
are interference restriction categories, then an
interference restriction functor

\mathcal{F}:(\mathbb{X},\perp)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(\mathbb{Y},\perp)
is a restriction functor
\mathcal{F}:\mathbb{X}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{Y}
which preserves the restrictions zeroes,
\mathcal{F}(0)=0
, and also preserves the interference relation in the sense that if
f\perp g
, then
\mathcal{F}(f)\perp\mathcal{F}(g)
. If there is no confusion, we will simply write an interference restriction functor as
{\mathcal{F}:\mathbb{X}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{Y}}
and say that it is a
\perp
-restriction functor.

Proposition 4.10

For a
\perp
-restriction category
\mathbb{X}
,
\mathsf{DJ}\left[(\mathbb{X},\perp)\right]
is a disjoint
\perp
-restriction category. Moreover
\mathcal{I}:(\mathbb{X},\perp)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{DJ}\left[(\mathbb{X},\perp)\right]
, defined on objects as
\mathcal{I}(A)=A
and on maps
\mathcal{I}(f)=\downarrow\!\!\{f\}
, is a faithful
\perp
-restriction functor.

Proof:
We must first explain why this construction is well-defined. This follows from the fact that this construction is a subcategory of the infinite version of the join completion given in
[
21
, Sec 10]
1
1

1

In
[
21
, Sec 10]
, the construction is given for finite families, however it is not difficult to see that the constructions and results still hold for arbitrary families.
. First note that
e\leq 1_{A}
if and only if
e
is a restriction idempotent, and therefore
\downarrow\!\!\{1_{A}\}=\mathcal{O}(A)
, so identity maps are well-defined. Next, observe that if
F\subseteq\mathbb{X}(A,B)
and
G\subseteq\mathbb{X}(B,C)
are families of parallel
\perp
-maps, then by
[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.4]
and
[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.5]
respectively, we have that
FG=\{fg|~f\in F,g\in G\}\subseteq\mathbb{X}(A,C)
and
\overline{F}=\{\overline{f}|~f\in F\}\subseteq\mathbb{X}(A,A)
are as well. Then using the same arguments as in the proof of
[
21
, Prop 10.3]
, we have that if
S=\downarrow\!\!F
and
T=\downarrow\!\!G
, then
ST=\downarrow\!\!FG=\{fg|~f\in F,g\in G\}
and
\overline{S}=\downarrow\!\!\overline{F}
, so composition and restrictions are well-defined. Thus by similar arguments as in the proof of
[
21
, Prop 10.3]
, we have that
\mathsf{DJ}\left[(\mathbb{X},\perp)\right]
is a restriction category. Now since the only maps less than
0
is
0
, we have that
\downarrow\!\!\{0\}=\{0\}=Z
. Then it immediately follows that the
Z
s are indeed the restriction zeroes for
\mathsf{DJ}\left[(\mathbb{X},\perp)\right]
.

Now let us explain why
\perp
is an interference relation.

[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.0]

By Lemma
3.5
.(
i
), we have that for all
e\in\mathcal{O}(A)
,
e\perp 0
. Thus
\mathcal{O}(A)\perp\{0\}
.

[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.1]

Suppose that
S\perp T
, that is, all
f\in S
and
g\in T
, we have that
f\perp g
. Then by
[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.1]
we also have that
g\perp f
for all
g\in T
and
f\in S
. So
T\perp S
.

[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.2]

Suppose that
S\perp S
, that is, for all
f\in S
,
f\perp f
. But then by
[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.2]
,
f=0
for all
f\in S
. So
S=\{0\}
.

[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.3]

By the same arguments as in the proof of
[
21
, Prop 10.5]
, we get that
S\leq S^{\prime}
in
\mathsf{DJ}\left[(\mathbb{X},\perp)\right]
if and only if
S\subseteq S^{\prime}
. So now suppose that
S^{\prime}\perp T^{\prime}
, and also that
S\leq S^{\prime}
and
T\leq T^{\prime}
, or in other words,
S\subseteq S^{\prime}
and
T\subseteq T^{\prime}
. Then for all
f\in S
and
g\in T
, we also have that
f\in S^{\prime}
and
g\in T^{\prime}
, and thus
f\perp g
. So
S\perp T
.

[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.4]

Suppose that
S\perp T
, that is, all
f\in S
and
g\in T
, we have that
f\perp g
. Now given suitable
U=\downarrow H
and
V=\downarrow K
, consider
USV
and
UTV
. Then a
x\in USV
is of the form
ufv
for some
u\in U
,
f\in S
, and
v\in V
, and similarly a
y\in UTV
is of the form
x=u^{\prime}gv^{\prime}
for some
u^{\prime}\in U
,
g\in T
, and
v^{\prime}\in V
. Now since
u,u^{\prime}\in U
, there are
h,h^{\prime}\in H
such that
u\leq h
and
u^{\prime}\leq h^{\prime}
. If
h\neq h^{\prime}
, then
h\perp h^{\prime}
, and then by
[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.3]
, we have that
u\perp u^{\prime}
. So by
[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.4]
, we get that
x=ufv\perp u^{\prime}gv^{\prime}=y
. Now consider instead if
h=h^{\prime}
. Now since
f\perp g
, by
[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.4]
we get
hfv\perp hgv^{\prime}
. Then since
u\leq h
and
u\prime\leq h
, we get that
ufv\leq hfv
and
u^{\prime}gv^{\prime}\leq h^{\prime}gv^{\prime}
, so by
[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.3]
we get that
x=ufv\perp u^{\prime}gv^{\prime}=y
. So
USV\perp UTV
.

[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.5]

Suppose that
S\perp T
, that is, all
f\in S
and
g\in T
, that
f\perp g
. Then by
[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.5]
we also have that
\overline{f}\perp\overline{g}
for all
\overline{f}\in\overline{S}
and
\overline{g}\in\overline{T}
. So
\overline{S}\perp\overline{T}
.

Thus
\mathsf{DJ}\left[(\mathbb{X},\perp)\right]
is a
\perp
-restriction category. It remains to discuss the disjoint joins. Now given a family of families of parallel
\perp
-disjoint maps
F_{i}\subseteq\mathbb{X}(A,B)
such that
F_{i}\perp F_{j}
for all
i\neq j
(that is,
f\perp g
for all
f\in F_{i}
and
g\in F_{j}
), then their union
\bigcup\limits_{i\in I}F_{i}\subseteq\mathbb{X}(A,B)
is also a family of pairwise
\perp
-disjoint parallel maps. Then by the same arguments as in the proof of
[
21
, Prop 10.5]
, we see that for a family of pairwise
\perp
-disjoint maps
S_{i}=\downarrow\!\!F_{i}
, that
\bigsqcup\limits_{i\in I}S_{i}=\downarrow\left(\bigcup\limits_{i\in I}F_{i}\right)
. By using again the same arguments as in the proof of
[
21
, Prop 10.5]
, we get that
[
\boldsymbol{\sqcup}
.1]
,
[
\boldsymbol{\sqcup}
.2]
, and
[
\boldsymbol{\sqcup}
.3]
hold. Finally, it remains to show
[
\boldsymbol{\sqcup}
.4]
. So consider family of pairwise
\perp
-disjoint maps
S_{i}
and another map
T
such that
S_{i}\perp T
for all
i\in I
. Then clearly
\bigcup\limits_{i\in I}S_{i}\perp T
as well, and so
[
\boldsymbol{\sqcup}
.4]
holds. So we conclude that
\mathsf{DJ}\left[(\mathbb{X},\perp)\right]
is a disjoint
\perp
-restriction category.

Finally, it remains to show that
\mathcal{I}
is an interference restriction functor. By the same arguments as in the proof of
[
21
, Thm 10.6]
, we get that
\mathcal{I}
is a faithful restriction functor. Moreover it preserves restriction zeroes since
\mathcal{I}(0)=\downarrow\!\!\{0\}=Z
. Now suppose that
f\perp g
in
(\mathbb{X},\perp)
. Then by
[
\boldsymbol{\sqcup}
.3]
, for all
f^{\prime}\leq f
and
g\prime\leq g
, we get that
f^{\prime}\perp g^{\prime}
. Thus
\mathcal{I}(f)=\downarrow\!\!\{f\}\perp\downarrow\!\!\{g\}=\mathcal{I}(g)
, and so we get that
\mathcal{I}
is indeed a
\perp
-restriction functor.

\Box

5
Kleene Wands

In this section, we introduce the notion of a
Kleene wand
for an interference restriction category with binary disjoint joins by adapting the definition of Kleene wand from
[
13
, Sec 4]
. The main result of this paper is that for an extensive restriction category, to have an iteration (or trace) operator on the coproduct is equivalent to having a Kleene wand. As such, the axioms of a Kleene wand are analogues to of that of an iteration operator. However, while iteration operators require the presence of coproducts, a Kleene wand can be defined without them. Thus Kleene wands capture iteration in settings without coproducts. An
itegory
is an interference restriction category with binary disjoint joins and Kleene wands. It is also worth noting that the definition of a Kleene wand in
[
13
, Sec 4]
was for the maximal interference relation
\perp_{0}
and in a setting with
restriction products

[
19
, Sec 4.1]
. So here we adapt some of the axioms for an arbitrary interference relation but do not consider having restriction products.

Definition 5.1

For a
\perp
-restriction category
\mathbb{X}
with all binary
\perp
-joins, a
Kleene wand

\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}
is a family of operators (indexed by pairs of objects
X,A\in\mathbb{X}
):

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}:\{(f,g)\in\mathbb{X}(X,X)\times\mathbb{X}(X,A)|f\perp g\}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{X}(X,A)

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}[c]f\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}g:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Af:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X~~~g:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A~~~f\perp g\end{matrix}

such that the following axioms hold:

[
\boldsymbol{\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-4.44444pt\cr$|$\cr}}}}
.1]

Iteration: For maps
f:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X
and
g:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
, if
f\perp g
, then
g\sqcup f(f\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
