. Also, for a family of maps
g_{1}:A_{1}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B_{1}
, …,
g_{n}:A_{n}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B_{n}
, define their coproduct
g_{1}+\ldots+g_{n}:A_{1}+\ldots+A_{n}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B_{1}+\ldots+B_{n}
as
g_{1}+\ldots+g_{n}\colon=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}g_{1}\iota_{1}\\
\vdots\\
g_{n}\iota_{n}\end{smallmatrix}\right]
. This choice of writing the copairing operation as a vertical column is justified by the matrix calculus of an extensive restriction category, which we review in the next section. We also denote the canonical codiagonal maps as
\nabla:=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}1_{A}\\
\vdots\\
1_{A}\end{smallmatrix}\right]:A+\ldots+A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
.

Definition 7.1

A
coCartesian restriction category
is a restriction category
\mathbb{X}
with finite
restriction coproducts

[
19
, Sec 2.1]
, that is,
\mathbb{X}
has finite coproducts where all the injection maps
{\iota_{j}:A_{j}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A_{1}+\ldots+A_{n}}
are total.

In a coCartesian restriction category with restriction zeroes, we can define “quasi-projections” for the restriction coproduct which make the injections into
restriction isomorphisms
(also known as
partial isomorphisms
). Recall that in a restriction category, a
restriction isomorphism

[
17
, Sec 2.3.1]
is a map
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
such that there is a map
f^{\circ}:B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
, called its
restriction inverse
, such that
ff^{\circ}=\overline{f}
and
f^{\circ}f=\overline{f^{\circ}}
. Restriction inverses are unique, and if
f
is a restriction isomorphism then its restriction inverse
f^{\circ}
is also a restriction isomorphism, whose restriction inverse is
f

[
17
, Lem 2.18.(vii)]
.

Definition 7.2

In a coCartesian restriction categories with restriction zeroes, for a finite family of objects
A_{0},\ldots,A_{n}
, the
quasi-projections
are the maps
\iota^{\circ}_{j}:A_{0}+\ldots+A_{n}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A_{j}
defined as the copairing of zeroes and the identity in the
j
th argument,
\iota^{\circ}_{j}=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
\vdots\\
0\\
1_{A_{j}}\\
0\\
\vdots\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right]
. In other words,
\iota^{\circ}_{j}
is the unique map such that
\iota_{j}\iota^{\circ}_{j}=1_{A_{j}}
and
\iota_{i}\iota^{\circ}_{j}=0
for
i\neq j
.

Other useful identities in coCartesian restriction categories with restriction zeroes can be found in
[
19
, Lemma 2.10]
and
[
20
, Lemma 3.6]
. In particular, as mentioned, the injection maps are restriction isomorphisms and also the initial object
\mathsf{0}
becomes a zero object, where to the unique map to and from
\mathsf{0}
are precisely the restriction zero maps.

As the name suggests, extensive restriction categories are the appropriate restriction category analogue of extensive categories
[
8
]
. More explicitly, an extensive restriction category is a restriction category with restriction coproducts and restriction zeroes, and such that every map whose codomain is a coproduct admits a
decision
.

Definition 7.3

An
extensive restriction category

[
19
, Sec 3]
is a coCartesian restriction category with restriction zeroes such that for every map
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B_{1}+\ldots+B_{n}
there exists a map
{\langle f\rangle:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\underbrace{A+\ldots+A}_{n\text{-times}}}
such that the following equalities hold:

[D.1]

\overline{f}=\langle f\rangle\nabla

[D.2]

\langle f\rangle\left(f+\ldots+f\right)=f\left(\iota_{1}+\ldots+\iota_{n}\right)

or equivalently if
\langle f\rangle
satisfies the following:

[D.3]

\langle f\rangle
is a restriction inverse of
{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\iota^{\circ}_{1}f\\
\vdots\\
\iota^{\circ}_{n}f\end{smallmatrix}\right]}:A+\ldots+A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A

[D.4]

\overline{\langle f\rangle}=\overline{f}
.

The map
\langle f\rangle
is called the
decision

[
19
, Prop 2.11]
of
f
.

To help keep track of types, it may be useful to write down
[D.1]
and
[D.2]
as commutative diagrams (for clarity we write brackets in the bottom corner):

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.31248pt\hbox{\kern 6.46802pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.46802pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 27.326pt\raise 8.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\langle f\rangle}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 66.46802pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 37.24693pt\raise-21.68263pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.83888pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\overline{f}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 82.93599pt\raise-29.40096pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 66.46802pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A+\ldots+A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 89.404pt\raise-16.08124pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.1525pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\nabla}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 89.404pt\raise-24.85pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 193.14108pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 261.73393pt\raise 8.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\langle f\rangle}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 335.67476pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 189.10286pt\raise-14.65623pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.575pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 199.6091pt\raise-23.99998pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 335.67476pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A+\ldots+A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 358.61075pt\raise-15.65623pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f+\ldots+f}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 358.61075pt\raise-23.99998pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-31.6875pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 82.93599pt\raise-31.6875pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 172.34pt\raise-31.6875pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B_{1}+\ldots+B_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 238.32254pt\raise-39.1875pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\iota_{1}+\ldots+\iota_{n}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 286.8782pt\raise-31.6875pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 286.8782pt\raise-31.6875pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(B_{1}+\ldots+B_{n})+\ldots+(B_{1}+\ldots+B_{n})}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

where for the bottom arrow on the right, it is the coproduct of each injections
\iota_{j}:B_{j}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B_{1}+\ldots+B_{n}
. Moreover, note that by
[D.3]
, since restriction inverses are unique, the decision
\langle f\rangle
is the unique map which satisfies
[D.1]
and
[D.2]
.

Intuitively, in an extensive restriction category, the restriction coproduct should be thought as a disjoint union. So then given a map
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B_{0}+\ldots+B_{n}
, when
f(x)
is defined, it lands in only one of the
B_{j}
. As such, the decision of
f
should be thought of as separating out where
f
is defined in the appropriate place. So intuitively, if
f(x)\uparrow
then
\langle f\rangle(x)\uparrow
, and when
f(x)\downarrow
and
f(x)\in B_{j}
, then
\langle f\rangle
sends
x
to
x
in the
j
th copy of
A
in the codomain. So when
f
is total this gives a decision procedure. This intuition is captured by our main example of an extensive restriction category in Ex
7.6
below. We can also consider maps that are their own decision.

Definition 7.4

In an extensive restriction category, a
decision

[
19
, Prop 2.15]
is map
d:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\underbrace{A+\ldots+A}_{n\text{-times}}
such that
\langle d\rangle=d
, in other words,
d
satisfies:

[d.1]

\overline{d}=d\nabla

[d.2]

d\left(d+\ldots+d\right)=d\left(\iota_{1}+\ldots+\iota_{n}\right)

Again, to help keep track of types, it may be useful to write down
[d.1]
and
[d.2]
as commutative diagrams (for clarity we will write brackets in the bottom corner):

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.31248pt\hbox{\kern 6.46802pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.46802pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 31.44423pt\raise 5.1875pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.1875pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{d}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 66.46802pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 37.24693pt\raise-21.68263pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.83888pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\overline{d}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 82.93599pt\raise-29.40096pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 66.46802pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A+\ldots+A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 89.404pt\raise-16.08124pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.1525pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\nabla}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 89.404pt\raise-24.85pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 188.80797pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 255.01936pt\raise 5.1875pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.1875pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{d}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 318.3423pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 185.2284pt\raise-14.65623pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.1875pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{d}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 195.27599pt\raise-23.99998pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 318.3423pt\raise 341.27829pt\raise-15.65623pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{d+\ldots+d}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 341.27829pt\raise-23.99998pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-31.6875pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 82.93599pt\raise-31.6875pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 172.34pt\raise-31.6875pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A+\ldots+A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 229.65631pt\raise-39.1875pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\iota_{1}+\ldots+\iota_{n}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 278.21198pt\raise-31.6875pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 278.21198pt\raise-31.6875pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(A+\ldots+A)+\ldots+(A+\ldots+A)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

where for the bottom arrow on the right, it is the coproduct of each injections
\iota_{j}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A+\ldots+A
.

Of course, for every suitable
f
, its decision
\langle f\rangle
is indeed a decision, so
\langle\langle f\rangle\rangle=\langle f\rangle

[
19
, Prop 2.15]
. Equivalently, a decision can also be characterize as a restriction isomorphism whose restriction inverse is the copairing of restriction idempotents.

Lemma 7.5

[
19
, Prop 2.15]
In an extensive restriction category,

(i)

A map
d:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A+\ldots+A
is a decision if and only if
d
is a restriction isomorphism such that for its restriction inverse
d^{\circ}:A+\ldots+A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
,
\iota_{j}d^{\circ}
are restriction idempotents.

(ii)

If
d
is a decision, then it is a restriction isomorphism with restriction inverse
d^{\circ}=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}d\iota^{\circ}_{1}\\
\vdots\\
d\iota^{\circ}_{n}\end{smallmatrix}\right]
.

As such, intuitively, an
n
-ary decision of type
A
should be interpreted as a collection of
n
disjoint subsets of
A
, as is best seen in our main example below.

Example 7.6

\mathsf{PAR}
is an extensive restriction category where the coproduct is given by disjoint union, which we denote as
X_{1}+\ldots+X_{n}=X_{1}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup X_{n}=\{(x,k)|~1\leq k\leq n,x\in X_{k}\}
, the initial object is the empty set
\mathsf{0}=\emptyset
, and for a partial function
f:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y_{1}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup Y_{n}
, its decision is the partial function
{\langle f\rangle:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X\sqcup\ldots\sqcup X}
defined as follows:

\langle f\rangle(x)=\begin{cases}(x,1)&\text{if }f(x)\downarrow\text{ and }f(x)\in Y_{1}\\
\vdots\\
(x,n)&\text{if }f(x)\downarrow\text{ and }f(x)\in Y_{n}\\
\uparrow&\text{if }f(x)\uparrow\end{cases}

Decisions in
\mathsf{PAR}
correspond to a finite family of disjoint subsets. Indeed, given a set
X
, an
n
-ary decision
{d:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X\sqcup\ldots\sqcup X}
induces
n
subsets of
X
defined as
d_{k}=\{x\in X|d(x)=(x,k)\}
for all
1\leq k\leq n
(which is well-defined since
d=\langle d\rangle
). Moreover, clearly for any
1\leq i,j\leq n
with
i\neq j
we have that
d_{i}\cap d_{j}=\emptyset
. On the other hand, given a finite family of subsets
U_{1},\ldots,U_{n}\in X
which are pairwise disjoint, that is,
U_{i}\cap U_{j}=\emptyset
when
i\neq j
, we obtain a decision
d_{U_{1},\ldots,U_{n}}:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X\sqcup\ldots\sqcup X
defined as:

d_{U_{1},\ldots,U_{n}}(x)=\begin{cases}(x,1)&\text{if }x\in U_{1}\\
\vdots\\
(x,n)&\text{if }x\in U_{n}\\
\uparrow&\text{if }x\notin U_{i}\text{ for all $1\leq i\leq n$}\end{cases}

Example 7.7

Another source of examples of extensive restriction categories worth mentioning are
distributive
restriction categories with restriction zeroes. Briefly, a
distributive restriction category

[
19
, Sec 5.3]
is a coCartesian restriction category which also has
restriction products

\times
and a
restriction terminal object

\mathsf{1}

[
19
, Sec 4.1]
2
2

2

It is important to recall that while restriction coproducts are the indeed usual coproducts, restriction products are not products in the usual sense. For more details on restriction products, we invite the reader to see
[
19
,
20
,
12
]
.
which distributes over the restriction coproducts, in the sense that
A\times(B+C)\cong(A\times B)+(A\times C)
. Every distributive restriction category with restriction zeroes is an extensive restriction category
[
19
, Thm 5.8]
, where for a map
{f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B_{0}+\ldots+B_{n}}
, its decision
{\langle f\rangle:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A+\ldots+A}
is defined as the following composite:

\begin{gathered}\begin{array}[c]{c}\langle f\rangle\end{array}:=\begin{array}[c]{c}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.31248pt\hbox{\kern 6.46802pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.46802pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 20.85521pt\raise 8.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\left\langle 1_{A},f\right\rangle}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 63.46802pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 63.46802pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\times\left(B_{0}+\ldots+B_{n}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 137.19719pt\raise 7.7pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.3pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{1_{A}\times\left(t_{B_{0}}+\ldots+t_{B_{n}}\right)}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 198.3866pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 198.3866pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\times(\mathsf{1}+\ldots+\mathsf{1})\cong A+\ldots+A}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\end{array}\end{gathered}

(14)

where
\langle-,-\rangle
is the pairing operation for the restriction product, and
t_{B_{i}}:B_{i}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{1}
is the unique total map to the restriction terminal object. As such, every classical distributive restriction category
[
20
, Sec 6]
(or equivalently any Kleisli category of the exception monad of a distributive category
[
20
, Sec 7]
) is an extensive restriction category as well. For more details on (classical) distributive restriction categories, we invite the curious reader to see
[
19
,
20
]
.

We now turn our attention to showing that every extensive restriction category has a canonical interference relation induced by decisions. The natural notion of maps being disjoint in an extensive restriction category is if they can separated by a binary decision.

Definition 7.8

In an extensive restriction category, we say that maps
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
and
{g:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}C}
are
decision disjoint
, written as
f\perp_{d}g
, if there exists a decision
\langle f|g\rangle:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A+A
such that
\langle f|g\rangle\iota^{\circ}_{1}=\overline{f}
and
\langle f|g\rangle\iota^{\circ}_{2}=\overline{g}
. We call
\langle f|g\rangle
the
separating decision
of
f
and
g
.

Recall that a binary decision should intuitively be thought of as a pair of disjoint subsets. Thus two maps are decision disjoint if their domains are disjoint and if there is a decision which captures these two disjoint subsets. Being decision disjoint is also equivalent to asking that the copairing of the restrictions is a restriction isomorphism, whose restriction inverse will be the separating decision. From this, since restriction inverses are unique, we also get that separating decision are unique (which justifies saying
the
separating decision instead of
a
separating decision).

Lemma 7.9

In an extensive restriction category,

(i)

f\perp_{d}g
if and only if
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\overline{f}\\
\overline{g}\end{smallmatrix}\right]
is a restriction isomorphism.

(ii)

If
f\perp_{d}g
, then their separating decision
\langle f|g\rangle
is the restriction inverse of
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\overline{f}\\
\overline{g}\end{smallmatrix}\right]
.

(iii)

A separating decision, if it exists, is unique.

Proof:
Starting with (
i
). For the
\Rightarrow
direction, suppose that
f\perp_{d}g
, so we have a decision
\langle f|g\rangle
such that
\langle f|g\rangle\iota^{\circ}_{1}=\overline{f}
and
\langle f|g\rangle\iota^{\circ}_{2}=\overline{g}
. By Lemma
7.5
.(
ii
), we know that
\langle f|g\rangle
is a restriction isomorphism and its restriction inverse is
\langle f|g\rangle^{\circ}=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\langle f|g\rangle\iota^{\circ}_{1}\\
\langle f|g\rangle\iota^{\circ}_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right]=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\overline{f}\\
\overline{g}\end{smallmatrix}\right]
. Since restriction inverses are themselves restriction isomorphisms, we have that
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\overline{f}\\
\overline{g}\end{smallmatrix}\right]
is a restriction isomorphism. Conversely, for the
\Leftarrow
direction, suppose that
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\overline{f}\\
\overline{g}\end{smallmatrix}\right]
is a restriction isomorphism and call its restriction inverse
\langle f|g\rangle
. By Lemma
7.5
.(
i
), since
\iota_{1}\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\overline{f}\\
\overline{g}\end{smallmatrix}\right]=\overline{f}
and
\iota_{2}\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\overline{f}\\
\overline{g}\end{smallmatrix}\right]=\overline{g}
are restriction idempotents, it follows that
\langle f|g\rangle
is a decision. Thus by Lemma
7.5
.(
ii
), we get that
\langle f|g\rangle
is a restriction isomorphism whose restriction inverse is
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\langle f|g\rangle\iota^{\circ}_{1}\\
\langle f|g\rangle\iota^{\circ}_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right]
. However by assumption, we know that since
\langle f|g\rangle
is the restriction inverse of
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\overline{f}\\
\overline{g}\end{smallmatrix}\right]
, that
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\overline{f}\\
\overline{g}\end{smallmatrix}\right]
is the restriction inverse of
\langle f|g\rangle
. Then since restriction inverses are unique, we get that
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\langle f|g\rangle\iota^{\circ}_{1}\\
\langle f|g\rangle\iota^{\circ}_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right]=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\overline{f}\\
\overline{g}\end{smallmatrix}\right]
. Thus it follows that
\langle f|g\rangle\iota^{\circ}_{1}=\overline{f}
and
\langle f|g\rangle\iota^{\circ}_{2}=\overline{g}
. So
f\perp_{d}g
as desired.

Then (
ii
) follows from what we’ve just shown. Lastly for (
iii
), since restriction inverse are unique, separating decision are unique as well.

\Box

We now show that decision separation is an interference relation and that extensive restriction categories have all binary disjoint joins.

Theorem 7.10

Let
\mathbb{X}
be an extensive restriction category, then
\perp_{d}
is an interference relation, making
\mathbb{X}
into a
\perp_{d}
-restriction category which has all binary
\perp_{d}
-joins.

Proof:
To prove that
\perp_{d}
is an interference relation, we will prove that
\perp_{d}
is a restrictional interference relation. Explicitly, the restriction idempotent version of
\perp_{d}
is given as follows: for restriction idempotents
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
,
e_{1}\perp_{d}e_{2}
if there exists a decision
\langle e_{1}|e_{2}\rangle:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A+A
such that
\langle e_{1}|e_{2}\rangle\iota^{\circ}_{1}=e_{1}
and
\langle e_{1}|e_{2}\rangle\iota^{\circ}_{2}=e_{2}
. Equivalently by Lemma
7.9
.(
i
),
e_{1}\perp_{d}e_{2}
if and only if
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}e_{1}\\
e_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right]
is a restriction isomorphism. We will make use of both descriptions.

[
\mathcal{O}\!\boldsymbol{\perp}
.0]

Recall that
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}1_{A}\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right]=\iota^{\circ}_{1}
is the quasi-projection, which we know is a restriction isomorphism. So
1_{A}\perp 0
, and moreover
\langle 1_{A}|0\rangle=\iota_{0}
.

[
\mathcal{O}\!\boldsymbol{\perp}
.1]

Suppose that
e_{1}\perp_{d}e_{2}
, which implies that
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}e_{1}\\
e_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right]
is a restriction isomorphism. First consider the canonical symmetry isomorphism of the coproduct,
\sigma:=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\iota_{2}\\
\iota_{1}\end{smallmatrix}\right]
, which is a restriction isomorphism (since every isomorphism is a restriction isomorphism). Now observe that
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}e_{2}\\
e_{1}\end{smallmatrix}\right]=\sigma\left[\begin{smallmatrix}e_{1}\\
e_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right]
. Then
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}e_{2}\\
e_{1}\end{smallmatrix}\right]
is the composite of restriction isomorphisms, and so is itself a restriction isomorphism. So
e_{2}\perp_{d}e_{1}
, and moreover
\langle e_{2}|e_{1}\rangle=\langle e_{1}|e_{2}\rangle\sigma
.

[
\mathcal{O}\!\boldsymbol{\perp}
.2]

Suppose that
e\perp_{d}e
. Then we have a decision
\langle e|e\rangle
such that
\langle e|e\rangle\iota^{\circ}_{1}=e
and
\langle e|e\rangle\iota^{\circ}_{2}=e
. Now recall that by using
[R.4]
, it follows that for any map
h
that
he=\overline{he}h
. As such, using that restriction idempotents are idempotent, the naturality of the quasi-projections, and
[d.2]
, we can compute that:

e=ee=\langle e|e\rangle\iota^{\circ}_{1}\langle e|e\rangle\iota^{\circ}_{2}=\langle e|e\rangle\left(\langle e|e\rangle+\langle e|e\rangle\right)\iota^{\circ}_{1}\iota^{\circ}_{2}=\langle e|e\rangle(\iota_{1}+\iota_{2})\iota^{\circ}_{1}\iota^{\circ}_{2}=\langle e|e\rangle\iota^{\circ}_{1}\iota_{1}\iota^{\circ}_{2}=\langle e|e\rangle\iota^{\circ}_{1}0=0

So
e=0
.

[
\mathcal{O}\!\boldsymbol{\perp}
.3]

Suppose that
e^{\prime}_{1}\perp_{d}e^{\prime}_{2}
, so
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}e^{\prime}_{1}\\
e^{\prime}_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right]
is a restriction isomorphism, and also suppose that
e_{1}\leq e_{1}^{\prime}
and
e_{2}\leq e^{\prime}_{2}
. First observe that from these inequalities, we get that
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}e_{1}\\
e_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right]=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}e_{1}e^{\prime}_{1}\\
e_{2}e_{2}^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\right]=(e_{1}+e_{2})\left[\begin{smallmatrix}e^{\prime}_{1}\\
e^{\prime}_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right]
. So
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}e_{1}\\
e_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right]=(e_{1}+e_{2})\left[\begin{smallmatrix}e^{\prime}_{1}\\
e^{\prime}_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right]
. However note that
e_{1}+e_{2}
is a restriction idempotent, and therefore also a restriction isomorphism. As such
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}e_{1}\\
e_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right]
is the composite of restriction isomorphisms, and so is itself a restriction isomorphism. So
e_{0}\perp_{d}e_{1}
, and moreover
\langle e_{1}|e_{2}\rangle=\langle e^{\prime}_{1}|e^{\prime}_{2}\rangle(e_{1}+e_{2})
.

[
\mathcal{O}\!\boldsymbol{\perp}
.4]

Suppose that
e_{1}\perp_{d}e_{2}
, which implies we have a decision
\langle e_{1}|e_{2}\rangle:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A+A
such that
\langle e_{1}|e_{2}\rangle\iota^{\circ}_{1}=e_{1}
and
\langle e_{1}|e_{2}\rangle\iota^{\circ}_{2}=e_{2}
. Given a map
h:A^{\prime}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
, consider the map
h\langle e_{1}|e_{2}\rangle:A^{\prime}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A+A
. Then define
\langle\overline{he_{0}}|\overline{he_{1}}\rangle:=\left\langle h\langle e_{1}|e_{2}\rangle\right\rangle:A^{\prime}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A^{\prime}+A^{\prime}
. By construction
\langle\overline{he_{0}}|\overline{he_{1}}\rangle
is decision. So it remains to show that it is the desired separating decision. To do so, we note that
\langle f\rangle\iota^{\circ}_{j}=\overline{f\iota^{\circ}_{j}}

[
19
, Proof of Prop 2.15]
. Using this, we can compute that:

\langle\overline{he_{1}}|\overline{he_{2}}\rangle\iota^{\circ}_{1}=\left\langle h\langle e_{1}|e_{2}\rangle\right\rangle\iota^{\circ}_{1}=\overline{h\langle e_{2}|e_{2}\rangle\iota^{\circ}_{1}}=\overline{he_{1}}

So
\langle\overline{he_{1}}|\overline{he_{2}}\rangle\iota^{\circ}_{1}=\overline{he_{1}}
, and similarly
\langle\overline{he_{1}}|\overline{he_{2}}\rangle\iota^{\circ}_{2}=\overline{he_{2}}
. So we conclude that
\overline{he_{1}}\perp_{d}\overline{he_{2}}
.

So we have that
\perp_{d}
is a restrictional interference relation. Now this induces an interference relation
\perp_{d}
where
f\perp_{d}g
if and only if
\overline{f}\perp_{d}\overline{g}
, meaning that there exists a decision
\langle\overline{f}|\overline{g}\rangle
such that
\langle\overline{f}|\overline{g}\rangle\iota^{\circ}_{0}=\overline{f}
and
\langle\overline{f}|\overline{g}\rangle\iota^{\circ}_{1}=\overline{g}
. Then setting
\langle f|g\rangle:=\langle\overline{f}|\overline{g}\rangle
, we see that the induced
\perp_{d}
is defined in exactly the same way as in Def
7.8
. So
\perp_{d}
is indeed an interference relation.

We now show that we also have binary
\perp_{d}
-joins. So given a pair of parallel maps
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
and
g:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
which are
\perp_{d}
-disjoint, so
f\perp_{d}g
, define the map
f\sqcup g:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
as the following composite:

\begin{gathered}\begin{array}[c]{c}f\sqcup g\end{array}:=\begin{array}[c]{c}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.31248pt\hbox{\kern 6.46802pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.46802pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 22.60275pt\raise 8.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\left\langle f|g\right\rangle}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 63.46802pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 63.46802pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A+A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 112.91927pt\raise 5.8pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.575pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f+g}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 151.6456pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 151.6456pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B+B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 206.1086pt\raise 5.1525pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.1525pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\nabla}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 240.84607pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 240.84607pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\end{array}\end{gathered}

(15)

[
\boldsymbol{\sqcup}
.1]

Using Lemma
7.5
.(
ii
), it is easy to check that for a decision
d
that
\overline{d\iota^{\circ}_{j}}d=\overline{d\iota^{\circ}_{j}}\iota_{j}
. In particular, this implies that
\overline{f}\left\langle f|g\right\rangle=f
and
\overline{g}\left\langle f|g\right\rangle=g
. So using this fact, it follows that we can compute:

\overline{f}(f\sqcup g)=\overline{f}\left\langle f|g\right\rangle(f+g)\nabla=\overline{f}\iota_{1}(f+g)\nabla=\overline{f}f\iota_{1}\nabla=f\iota_{1}\nabla=f

So
f\leq f\sqcup g
, and similarly we can also show that
g\leq f\sqcup g
.

[
\boldsymbol{\sqcup}
.2]

Suppose that we have another map
h
such that
f\leq h
and
g\leq h
. This also implies that
f+g\leq h+h
. As such, using
[d.1]
and the fact that if
e
is a restriction idempotent then
eh\leq h
, we can compute that:

f\sqcup g=\left\langle f|g\right\rangle(f+g)\nabla\leq\left\langle f|g\right\rangle(h+h)\nabla=\left\langle f|g\right\rangle\nabla h=\overline{\left\langle f|g\right\rangle}h\leq h

So
f\sqcup g\leq h
.

[
\boldsymbol{\sqcup}
.3]

Consider a map
k:A^{\prime}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
. By the proof of
[
\mathcal{O}\!\boldsymbol{\perp}
.4]
above, it follows that
\langle kf|kg\rangle=\langle k\langle f|g\rangle\rangle
. Next we compute that:

\displaystyle\langle k\langle f|g\rangle\rangle(k+k)(f+g)=~\langle k\langle f|g\rangle\rangle(k+k)(\overline{f}+\overline{g})(f+g)

(
[R.1]
)

\displaystyle=~\langle k\langle f|g\rangle\rangle(k+k)(\langle f|g\rangle+\langle f|g\rangle)(\iota^{\circ}_{1}+\iota^{\circ}_{2})(f+g)

(Sep. Dec.)

\displaystyle=~k\langle f|g\rangle(\iota_{1}+\iota_{2})(\iota^{\circ}_{1}+\iota^{\circ}_{2})(f+g)

(
[D.2]
)

\displaystyle=~k\langle f|g\rangle(f+g)

(Def. of
\iota^{\circ}_{j}
)

In other words, we have that
\langle kf|kg\rangle(kf+kg)=k\langle f|g\rangle(f+g)
. From this we can easily compute that:

kf\sqcup kg=\left\langle kf|kg\right\rangle(kf+kg)\nabla=k\langle f|g\rangle(f+g)\nabla=k\left(f\sqcup g\right)

So
kf\sqcup kg=k\left(f\sqcup g\right)
as desired.

So we conclude that an extensive restriction category is a
\perp_{d}
-restriction category with all binary
\perp_{d}
-joins.

\Box

In general, however, extensive restriction categories may not have arbitrary finite
\perp_{d}
-joins, since
\perp_{d}
-joins are not necessarily strong. The issue is that for a finite family of pairwise
\perp_{d}
-disjoint maps, even if pairwise we have binary separating decision for them, to build their join one really requires an
n
-ary separating decision.

Definition 7.11

In an extensive restriction category
\mathbb{X}
, a finite family of maps
\{f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}\}
that have the same domain
A
are said to be
decision disjoint
if there exists an
n
-ary decision
\langle f_{1}|\ldots|f_{n}\rangle:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\underbrace{A+\ldots+A}_{n\text{-times}}
such that
\langle f_{1}|\ldots|f_{n}\rangle\iota^{\circ}_{j}=\overline{f_{j}}
. We call
\langle f_{1}|\ldots|f_{n}\rangle
the
separating decision
of the
f_{j}
s.

Of course, similarly to the binary case, we can also say being decision disjoint is equivalent to asking that the copairing of the restrictions is a restriction isomorphism. Moreover, being
n
-ary decision disjoint implies that they are pairwise binary decision disjoint.

Lemma 7.12

In an extensive restriction category,

(i)

\{f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}\}
are decision disjoint if and only if
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\overline{f_{1}}\\
\vdots\\
\overline{f_{n}}\end{smallmatrix}\right]
is a restriction isomorphism.

(ii)

If
\{f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}\}
are decision disjoint, then their separating decision
\langle f_{1}|\ldots|f_{n}\rangle
is the restriction inverse of
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\overline{f_{1}}\\
\vdots\\
\overline{f_{n}}\end{smallmatrix}\right]
.

(iii)

A separating decision, if it exists, is unique.

(iv)

If
\{f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}\}
are decision disjoint, then
\{f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}\}
is also a family of pairwise
\perp_{d}
-disjoint maps, that is,
f_{i}\perp_{d}f_{j}
for all
i\neq j
.

(v)

The quasi-projections
{\iota^{\circ}_{j}:A_{1}+\ldots+A_{n}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A_{j}}
are decision disjoint and therefore pairwise
\perp
-disjoint.

Proof:
Parts (
i
), (
ii
), and (
iii
) are
n
-ary versions of Lemma
7.9
.(
i
), (
ii
), and (
iii
) respectively, so they are proven in similar fashion. For (
iv
), suppose
\{f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}\}
are decision disjoint, so we have an
n
-ary separating decision
\langle f_{1}|\ldots|f_{n}\rangle
. Now let
\iota^{\circ}_{i,j}:A_{1}+\ldots+A_{n}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A_{i}+A_{j}
be the obvious quasi-projection which picks out the
A_{i}
and
A_{j}
components. Then for
f_{i}
and
f_{j}
, with
i\neq j
, define
\langle f_{i}|f_{j}\rangle=\{f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}\}\iota^{\circ}_{i,j}
. It is straightforward to check that
\langle f_{i}|f_{j}\rangle
is indeed a binary separating decision for
f_{i}
and
f_{j}
, and therefore we have that
f_{i}\perp_{d}f_{j}
as desired. Lastly for (
v
), it is straightforward to see that the quasi-projections
\{\iota^{\circ}_{1},\ldots,\iota^{\circ}_{n}\}
are decision disjoint, with their separating decision being
\iota_{1}+\ldots+\iota_{n}
. As such by (
iv
), the quasi-projection are indeed also a family of
\perp_{d}
-disjoint maps.

\Box

It is again worth stressing that the converse of Lemma
7.12
.(
iv
) is not necessarily true. Indeed, even if
\{f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}\}
are pairwise
\perp_{d}
-disjoint, they are not necessarily decision disjoint since we cannot in general build the necessary
n
-ary separating decision from the binary ones. However, as mentioned above, we do get disjoint joins of decision disjoint maps by the using the
n
-ary decision separation. So while we may not have all finite
\perp_{d}
-joins, we do get disjoint joins of a particular class of
\perp_{d}
-disjoint maps, the ones that are also decision disjoint.

Lemma 7.13

In an extensive restriction category, for a finite family of parallel maps
\{f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}|~f_{i}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B\}
which are decision disjoint, their
\perp_{d}
-join exists and is defined as follows:

\begin{gathered}\begin{array}[c]{c}f_{1}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup f_{n}\end{array}:=\begin{array}[c]{c}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.36113pt\hbox{\kern 6.46802pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.46802pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.4557pt\raise 8.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\left\langle f_{1}|\ldots|f_{n}\right\rangle}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 63.46802pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 63.46802pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A+\ldots+A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 117.71663pt\raise 7.5pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f_{1}+\ldots+f_{n}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 166.34pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 166.34pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B+\ldots+B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 235.49739pt\raise 5.1525pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.1525pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\nabla}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 270.23486pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 270.23486pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\end{array}\end{gathered}

(16)

Moreover, composition preserves these joins in the sense that Lemma
4.3
.(
iii
) holds.

Proof:
This is shown in similar way to how the binary case was shown in the proof of Thm
7.10
. So we leave this as an excercise for the reader.

\Box

With all that said, in many examples of extensive restriction categories,
\perp_{d}
-joins are in fact strong. When this is the case, the converse of Lemma
7.12
.(
iv
) is true.

Lemma 7.14

In an extensive restriction category such that finite
\perp_{d}
-join are strong, then
\{f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}\}
are decision disjoint if and only if
\{f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}\}
are pairwise
\perp_{d}
-disjoint.

Proof:
The
\Rightarrow
direction is Lemma
7.12
.(
iv
). For the
\Leftarrow
direction, suppose that
\{f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}\}
are pairwise
\perp_{d}
-disjoint. Then by
[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.4]
and
[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.5]
, we also get that
\{\overline{f_{1}}\iota_{1},\ldots,\overline{f_{n}}\iota_{n}\}
are pairwise
\perp_{d}
-disjoint. Then we can take their join, and so define
\langle f_{1}|\ldots|f_{n}\rangle:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A+\ldots+A
as follows:

\displaystyle\langle f_{1}|\ldots|f_{n}\rangle:=\overline{f}\iota_{1}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup\overline{f}\iota_{n}

(17)

We show that this is a separating decision by showing that it is the restriction inverse of
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\overline{f_{1}}\\
\vdots\\
\overline{f_{n}}\end{smallmatrix}\right]
. So we first compute that (recall that in a restriction category, if
y
is total then
\overline{xy}=\overline{x}
):

\displaystyle\langle f_{1}|\ldots|f_{n}\rangle\begin{bmatrix}\overline{f_{1}}\\
\vdots\\
\overline{f_{n}}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=~\left(\overline{f_{1}}\iota_{1}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup\overline{f_{n}}\iota_{n}\right)\begin{bmatrix}\overline{f}\\
\overline{g}\end{bmatrix}

(Def.)

\displaystyle=~\overline{f_{1}}\iota_{1}\begin{bmatrix}\overline{f_{1}}\\
\vdots\\
\overline{f_{n}}\end{bmatrix}\sqcup\ldots\overline{f_{n}}\iota_{n}\begin{bmatrix}\overline{f_{1}}\\
\vdots\\
\overline{f_{n}}\end{bmatrix}

(Lemma
4.3
.(
iii
))

\displaystyle=~\overline{f_{1}}~\overline{f_{1}}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup\overline{f_{n}}~\overline{f_{n}}

\displaystyle=~\overline{f_{1}}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup\overline{f_{n}}

(Rest. Idem.)

\displaystyle=~\overline{\overline{f_{1}}}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup\overline{\overline{f_{n}}}

(Rest. Idem.)

\displaystyle=~\overline{\overline{f_{1}}\iota_{1}}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup\overline{\overline{f_{n}}\iota_{n}}

(
\iota_{j}
total)

\displaystyle=~\overline{\overline{f_{1}}\iota_{1}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup\overline{f_{n}}\iota_{n}}

(Lemma
4.3
.(
ii
))

\displaystyle=~\overline{\langle f_{1}|\ldots|f_{n}\rangle}

(Def.)

Next we compute that:

\displaystyle\iota_{j}\begin{bmatrix}\overline{f_{1}}\\
\vdots\\
\overline{f_{n}}\end{bmatrix}\langle f_{1}|\ldots|f_{n}\rangle=\overline{f_{j}}\langle f_{1}|\ldots|f_{n}\rangle=\overline{f_{j}}\left(\overline{f_{1}}\iota_{1}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup\overline{f_{n}}\iota_{n}\right)=\overline{f_{j}}~\overline{f_{1}}\iota_{1}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup\overline{f_{j}}~\overline{f_{n}}\iota_{n}=0\sqcup\ldots\sqcup 0\sqcup\overline{f_{j}}\iota_{j}\sqcup 0\sqcup\ldots\sqcup 0=\overline{f_{j}}\iota_{j}

Then by the universal property of the coproduct, we get that
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\overline{f_{1}}\\
\vdots\\
\overline{f_{n}}\end{smallmatrix}\right]\langle f_{1}|\ldots|f_{n}\rangle=\overline{f_{1}}+\ldots+\overline{f_{n}}
. However, recall that the restriction of the copairing is the coproduct of the restrictions
[
19
, Lemma 2.1]
, so
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\overline{f_{1}}\\
\vdots\\
\overline{f_{n}}\end{smallmatrix}\right]\langle f_{1}|\ldots|f_{n}\rangle=\overline{f_{1}}+\ldots+\overline{f_{n}}=\overline{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\overline{f_{1}}\\
\vdots\\
\overline{f_{n}}\end{smallmatrix}\right]}
. So we conclude that
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\overline{f_{1}}\\
\vdots\\
\overline{f_{n}}\end{smallmatrix}\right]
is the restriction inverse of
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\overline{f_{1}}\\
\vdots\\
\overline{f_{n}}\end{smallmatrix}\right]
. Therefore by Lemma
7.12
.(
i
), we have that
\{f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}\}
are decision disjoint.

\Box

A natural question to ask is if an extensive restriction category can have more than one (non-trivial) interference relation. It turns out that under the mild natural assumption that the quasi-projection are pairwise disjoint and binary disjoint joins exist, the only interference relation which satisfies these assumptions is precisely the decision separation interference. As such, this justifies that
\perp_{d}
can indeed be considered as the canonical interference relation for an extensive restriction category.

Lemma 7.15

Let
\mathbb{X}
be an extensive restriction category. Suppose that
\mathbb{X}
admits an interference relation
\perp
such that all binary
\perp
-joins exist and such that for any finite family of object
A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}
, the quasi-projections
\iota^{\circ}_{i}:A_{1}+\ldots+A_{n}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A_{i}
are pairwise
\perp
-disjoint, that is,
\iota_{i}^{\circ}\perp\iota_{j}^{\circ}
for all
1\leq i,j\leq n
. Then
\perp=\perp_{d}
.

Proof:
Suppose that
f\perp g
. We need to show that
f
and
g
are also decision disjoint. To do so, note that since
f\perp g
, by
[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.4]
we also have that
f\iota_{1}\perp g\iota_{2}
. Since we have binary
\perp
-joins, we define the map
\langle f|g\rangle:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A+A
as
\langle f|g\rangle:=\overline{f}\iota_{1}\sqcup\overline{g}\iota_{1}
. We can show that
\langle f|g\rangle
and
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\overline{f}\\
\overline{g}\end{smallmatrix}\right]
are restriction inverses of each other by using similar calculations as in the proof of Lemma
7.14
. So by Lemma
7.9
.(
i
) and (
ii
), we get that
\langle f|g\rangle
is a separating decision for
f
and
g
, and thus
f\perp_{d}g
. So we get that
\perp\subseteq\perp_{d}
. Conversely, suppose that instead
f\perp_{d}g
. By assumption we have that
\iota^{\circ}_{1}\perp\iota^{\circ}_{2}
. By
[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.4]
, we get that
\overline{f}=\langle f|g\rangle\iota^{\circ}_{1}\perp\langle f|g\rangle\iota^{\circ}_{2}=\overline{g}
, and so
\overline{f}\perp\overline{g}
. Then by Lemma
3.2
, we get that
f\perp g
, and thus
\perp_{d}\subseteq\perp
. So we conclude that
\perp=\perp_{d}
.

\Box

In particular, we can consider the case when there are disjoint joins for the maximal interference relation.

Corollary 7.16

Let
\mathbb{X}
be an extensive restriction category such that
\mathbb{X}
is a finitely disjoint
\perp_{0}
-restriction category. Then
\perp_{0}=\perp_{d}
, and therefore being decision disjoint is equivalent to being pairwise
\perp_{0}
-disjoint.

Proof:
It is straightforward to check that the quasi-projections are pairwise
\perp_{0}
-disjoint. Then by applying the above lemma, we get that
\perp_{0}=\perp_{d}
.

\Box

As such, for many important examples of extensive restriction categories (such as the ones considered in
[
13
]
),
\perp_{d}=\perp_{0}
and so
\perp_{d}
-joins are in fact strong, which implies that we can build
n
-ary separating decisions from binary ones. This is also case for an extensive restriction category with all joins.

Example 7.17

Since
\mathsf{PAR}
has all
\perp_{0}
-joins, it follows that a family of partial functions
\{f_{1}:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y_{1},\ldots,f_{n}:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y_{n}\}
is decision disjoint precisely if they are all pairwise
\perp_{0}
-disjoint, which recall means that their domains of definitions are all pairwise disjoint,
\mathsf{dom}(f_{i})\cap\mathsf{dom}(f_{j})=\emptyset
for
i\neq j
. As such, their separating decision
{\langle f_{1}|\ldots|f_{n}\rangle:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X\sqcup\ldots\sqcup X}
is defined as follows:

\langle f_{1}|\ldots|f_{n}\rangle(x)=\begin{cases}(x,1)&\text{if }f_{1}(x)\downarrow\\
\vdots\\
(x,n)&\text{if }f_{n}(x)\downarrow\\
\uparrow&\text{if }f(x)\uparrow\end{cases}

Example 7.18

A classical distributive restriction category has all finite joins of compatible maps by definition. So in particular, a classical distributive restriction category has all finite
\perp_{0}
-joins, and so we get that being decision disjoint is equivalent to being pairwise
\perp_{0}
-disjoint.

8
Matrix Representation for Extensive Restriction Categories

In this section, we review the matrix calculus for an extensive restriction category as introduced in
[
19
]
. We will then provide a matrix construction on a finitely disjoint interference restriction category which produces an extensive restriction category.

In an extensive restriction category, every map of type
A_{1}+\ldots+A_{n}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B_{1}+\ldots+B_{m}
can be uniquely represented as an
n\times m
matrix where the
(i,j)
coordinate is a map of type
A_{i}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B_{j}
, and such that the rows are decision disjoint
[
19
, Thm 2.12]
. Explicitly, starting with a map of type
F:A_{1}+\ldots+A_{n}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B_{1}+\ldots+B_{m}
, the map
F_{i,j}:A_{i}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B_{j}
is defined by pre-composing
F
with the injection and post-composing with the quasi-projection:

\displaystyle F_{i,j}=\iota_{i}F\iota^{\circ}_{j}

Moreover, for all
1\leq i\leq n
, the family
\{F_{i,1},\ldots,F_{i,m}\}
is decision disjoint whose separating decision is defined as the decision of
\iota_{i}F:A_{i}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B_{1}+\ldots+B_{m}
, that is:

\displaystyle\langle F_{i,1}|\ldots|F_{i,m}\rangle:=\langle\iota_{i}F\rangle:A_{i}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A_{i}+\ldots+A_{i}

Conversely, given a family of maps
\{f_{i,j}:A_{i}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B_{j}|~1\leq i\leq n,1\leq j\leq m\}
such that for each
i
,
\{f_{i,1},\ldots,f_{i,m}\}
is decision disjoint, define the map
f:A_{1}+\ldots+A_{n}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B_{1}+\ldots+B_{m}
as the copairing:

\displaystyle f:=\begin{bmatrix}\langle f_{1,1}|\ldots|f_{1,m}\rangle(f_{1,1}+\ldots+f_{1,m})\\
\langle f_{2,1}|\ldots|f_{2,m}\rangle(f_{2,1}+\ldots+f_{2,m})\\
\vdots\\
\langle f_{n,1}|\ldots|f_{n,m}\rangle(f_{n,1}+\ldots+f_{n,m})\end{bmatrix}

These constructions are inverses of each other, see the proof of
[
19
, Thm 2.12]
for details.

Thus, when we have a map of type
f:A_{1}+\ldots+A_{n}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B_{1}+\ldots+B_{m}
, we can write it as a matrix,

f=\begin{bmatrix}f_{1,1}&f_{1,2}&\ldots&f_{1,m}\\
f_{2,1}&f_{2,2}&\ldots&f_{2,m}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ldots&\vdots\\
f_{n,1}&f_{n,2}&\ldots&f_{n,m}\end{bmatrix},

where the components are maps of type
f_{i,j}:A_{i}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B_{j}
, and the rows
\{f_{i,1},\ldots,f_{i,m}\}
(for a fixed
i
) are decision disjoint. By Lemma
7.12
.(
iv
), this also means that for each row, the component of a row are pairwise decision disjoint, that is,
f_{i,j}\perp_{d}f_{i,j^{\prime}}
for all
1\leq i\leq n
and
1\leq j,j^{\prime}\leq m
. Sometimes as a shorthand, we may simply write our matrices as
f=[f_{i,j}]_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq n\\
1\leq j\leq m\end{subarray}}
, or simply
f=[f_{i,j}]
when there is no confusion over the bounds of
i
and
j
.

Matrices of size
n\times 1
are column vectors: they correspond precisely to copairing, while matrices of size
1\times m
correspond precisely to a family of maps which are decision disjoint. On the other hand, composition in an extensive restriction category corresponds to matrix multiplication, where the disjoint join plays the role of the sum
[
19
, Prop 2.13]
. So given maps
f:A_{1}+\ldots+A_{n}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B_{1}+\ldots+B_{m}
and
g:B_{1}+\ldots+B_{m}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}C_{1}+\ldots+C_{p}
, the matrix representation of their composite
fg:A_{1}+\ldots+A_{n}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}C_{1}+\ldots+C_{p}
is given by:

\displaystyle(fg)_{i,k}=\bigsqcup\limits^{m}_{j=1}f_{i,j}g_{j,k}

\displaystyle fg=\left[\bigsqcup\limits^{m}_{j=1}f_{i,j}g_{j,k}\right]_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq n\\
1\leq k\leq p\end{subarray}}

(18)

Note that this is well-defined since for each
i
and
k
,
\{f_{i,j}g_{j,k}|1\leq j\leq m\}
are decision disjoint with separating decision:

\displaystyle\langle f_{i,1}g_{1,k}|\ldots|f_{i,m}g_{m,k}\rangle:=\left\langle\langle f_{i,1}|\ldots|f_{i,m}\rangle\left(f_{i,1}g_{1,k}+\ldots+f_{i,m}g_{m,k}\right)\right\rangle

(19)

Keen eyed readers may note that this formula is not exactly the same one given in
[
19
, Prop 2.13]
where
(fg)_{i,k}
is instead given by:

\displaystyle\langle f_{i,1}|\ldots|f_{i,m}\rangle\left(f_{i,1}g_{1,k}+\ldots+f_{i,m}g_{m,k}\right)\nabla

(20)

However, by
[D.2]
it follows that:

\displaystyle\langle f_{i,1}|\ldots|f_{i,m}\rangle\left(f_{i,1}g_{1,k}+\ldots+f_{i,m}g_{m,k}\right)=\langle f_{i,1}g_{1,k}|\ldots|f_{i,m}g_{m,k}\rangle\left(f_{i,1}g_{1,k}+\ldots+f_{i,m}g_{m,k}\right)

and thus the two formulas are the same.

With this matrix representation, decisions correspond precisely to row matrices of restriction idempotents. So if
d:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A+\ldots+A
is a decision, then it is a row matrix of the form:

d=\begin{bmatrix}e_{1}&\ldots&e_{n}\end{bmatrix}

where each
e_{i}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is a restriction idempotent.

We conclude this section by showing that every finitely disjoint interference restriction category embeds into an extensive restriction category via a matrix construction. This construction is essentially the same as one given in
[
21
, Sec 9]
, which was given for the maximal interference relation
\perp_{0}
: here we slightly generalize the construction to be for an arbitrary interference relation.

Definition 8.1

Let
\mathbb{X}
be a finitely disjoint
\perp
-restriction category. Define the extensive restriction category
\mathsf{MAT}\left[(\mathbb{X},\perp)\right]
as follows:

(i)

The objects of
\mathsf{MAT}\left[(\mathbb{X},\perp)\right]
are finite lists
(A_{1},\ldots,A_{n})
of objects
A_{i}
of
\mathbb{X}
, and including the empty list
()
;

(ii)

A map
F:(A_{1},\ldots,A_{n})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(B_{1},\ldots,B_{n})
in
\mathsf{MAT}\left[(\mathbb{X},\perp)\right]
is a
n\times m
matrix:

[F_{i,j}]_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq n\\
1\leq j\leq m\end{subarray}}

whose components are maps of type
F_{i,j}:A_{i}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B_{j}
and such that for each
1\leq i\leq n
, each row
\{F_{i,1},\ldots,F_{i,m}\}
is a
\perp
-disjoint family, that is,
F_{i,j}\perp F_{i,j^{\prime}}
for all
1\leq i\leq n
and
1\leq j,j^{\prime}\leq m
.

(iii)

The identity is the diagonal matrix

1_{(A_{1},\ldots,A_{n})}=Diag[1_{A_{1}},\ldots,1_{A_{n}}]

that is,
{1_{(A_{1},\ldots,A_{n})}}_{i,j}=0
if
i\neq j
and
{1_{(A_{1},\ldots,A_{n})}}_{i,i}=1_{A_{i}}
;

(iv)

Composition of
F:(A_{1},\ldots,A_{n})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(B_{1},\ldots,B_{n})
and
G:(B_{1},\ldots,B_{n})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(C_{1},\ldots,C_{p})
is defined as:

FG=\left[\bigsqcup\limits^{m}_{j=1}F_{i,j}G_{j,k}\right]_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq n\\
1\leq k\leq p\end{subarray}}:(A_{1},\ldots,A_{n})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(C_{1},\ldots,C_{p})

(v)

The restriction of
F:(A_{1},\ldots,A_{n})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(B_{1},\ldots,B_{m})
is defined as the diagonal matrix:

\overline{F}=Diag\left[\bigsqcup\limits^{m}_{j=1}\overline{F_{1,j}},\ldots,\bigsqcup\limits^{m}_{j=1}\overline{F_{n,j}}\right]:(A_{1},\ldots,A_{n})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A_{1},\ldots,A_{n})

that is,
\overline{F}_{i,j}=0
if
i\neq j
and
\overline{F}_{i,i}=\bigsqcup\limits^{m}_{j=1}\overline{F_{i,j}}
;

(vi)

The (binary) restriction coproduct is given by the concatenation of lists,

(A_{{}_{(}1,1)},\ldots,A_{(1,n_{1})})+\ldots+(A_{(m,1)},\ldots,A_{(m,n_{m})})=(A_{{}_{(}1,1)},\ldots,A_{(1,n_{1})},\ldots,A_{(m,1)},\ldots,A_{(m,n_{m})})

and where the injections:

I_{j}:(A_{(j,1)},\ldots,A_{(j,n_{j})})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A_{{}_{(}1,1)},\ldots,A_{(1,n_{1})})+\ldots+(A_{(m,1)},\ldots,A_{(m,n_{m})})

is defined as
{I_{j}}_{i,k}=0
if
k\neq i+j
and
{I_{j}}_{i,i+j}=1_{A_{(j,i)}}
;

(vii)

The restriction zero maps
0:(A_{1},\ldots,A_{n})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(B_{1},\ldots,B_{m})
are matrices with all zero components,
0_{i,j}=0
, and where the zero object is the empty list
()
.

Since we want to give an embedding, we also need to explain what we mean by a functor between (finitely) disjoint interference restriction categories. If
\mathbb{X}
and
\mathbb{Y}
are (finitely) disjoint
\perp
-interference restriction categories, then a
(finitely) disjoint
\perp
-interference restriction functor
is a
\perp
-restriction functor
\mathcal{F}:(\mathbb{X},\perp)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(\mathbb{Y},\perp)
which preserves the (finite)
\perp
-joins,
\mathcal{F}(\bigsqcup\limits_{i\in I}f_{i})=\bigsqcup\limits_{i\in I}\mathcal{F}(f_{i})
.

Proposition 8.2

For a finitely disjoint
\perp
-restriction category
\mathbb{X}
,
\mathsf{MAT}\left[(\mathbb{X},\perp)\right]
is an extensive restriction category, and furthermore:

(i)

All finite
\perp_{d}
-joins are strong, so
\mathsf{MAT}\left[(\mathbb{X},\perp)\right]
is also a finitely disjoint
\perp_{d}
-restriction category.

(ii)

F\perp_{d}G
if and only if
F_{i,j}\perp G_{i,k}
for all
i,j
and
k
.

(iii)

If
\{F_{1},\ldots,F_{n}\}
are pairwise
\perp_{d}
-disjoint parallel
n\times m
matrices, then
\bigsqcup\limits^{n}_{k=1}F_{k}=\left[\bigsqcup\limits^{n}_{k=1}{F_{k}}_{i,j}\right]_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq n\\
1\leq j\leq m\end{subarray}}
.

Furthermore,
\mathcal{I}:\mathbb{X}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{MAT}\left[(\mathbb{X},\perp)\right]
, defined on objects as
\mathcal{I}(A)=(A)
and on maps
\mathcal{I}(f)=[\mathcal{I}(f)_{1,1}]=[f]
(that is,
\mathcal{I}(f)
is a
1\times 1
whose only coefficient is
\mathcal{I}(f)_{1,1}=f
), is a finitely disjoint
\perp
-restriction functor.

Proof:
Let us first explain why this construction is well defined. Clearly, a row in either an identity matrix or an injection matrix is indeed
\perp
-disjoint by Lemma
3.5
.(
i
), which gives us
1\perp 0
and
0\perp 0
. Now given two composable matrices (omitting bounds for readability)
F=[F_{i,j}]
and
G=[G_{j,k}]
, since
F_{i,j}\perp F_{i,j^{\prime}}
, by
[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.4]
we get that
F_{i,j}G_{j,k}\perp F_{i,j^{\prime}}G_{j^{\prime},k}
. Then applying
[
\boldsymbol{\sqcup}
.4]
, we get that
\bigsqcup\limits^{m}_{j=1}F_{i,j}G_{j,k}\perp\bigsqcup\limits^{m}_{j=1}F_{i,j}G_{j,k^{\prime}}
, and so a row of
FG
is
\perp
-disjoint. Then by same arguments as in proofs of
[
21
, Prop 9.6 and Prop 9.9]
, we get that
\mathsf{MAT}\left[(\mathbb{X},\perp)\right]
is a coCartesian restriction category with restriction zeroes.

Next we need to explain why
\mathsf{MAT}\left[(\mathbb{X},\perp)\right]
is also extensive. However by
[
19
, Prop 2.18]
, it suffices to show that we have binary decisions. So for a matrix
F:(A_{1},\ldots,A_{n})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(B_{1},\ldots,B_{m})+(C_{1},\ldots,C_{p})
, define
\langle F\rangle:(A_{1},\ldots,A_{n})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A_{1},\ldots,A_{n})+(A_{1},\ldots,A_{n})
as the matrix
\langle F\rangle_{i,j}=0
for
j\neq i
or
i+n
,
\langle F\rangle_{i,i}=\bigsqcup\limits^{m}_{k=1}\overline{F_{i,k}}
, and
\langle F\rangle_{i,i+n}=\bigsqcup\limits^{p}_{k^{\prime}=1}\overline{F_{i,m+k^{\prime}}}
. This is well-defined since each row of
F
is a family
\perp
-disjoint maps, and so by
[
\boldsymbol{\perp}
.5]
, we get that
\{\overline{F_{i,1}},\ldots,\overline{F_{i,m}}\}
and
\{\overline{F_{i,m+1}},\ldots,\overline{F_{i,m+p}}\}
are also families of parallel
\perp
-disjoint maps, so we may their
\perp
-joins. Moreover, by
[
\boldsymbol{\sqcup}
.4]
we also get that
\bigsqcup\limits^{m}_{k=1}\overline{F_{i,k}}\perp\bigsqcup\limits^{p}_{k^{\prime}=1}\overline{F_{i,m+k^{\prime}}}
, and therefore it follows that each row of
\langle F\rangle
is also
\perp
-disjoint. Thus
\langle F\rangle
is indeed a map in
\mathsf{MAT}\left[(\mathbb{X},\perp)\right]
. Next we show that this is a decision for
F
.

[D.1]

First note that
\nabla:(A_{1},\ldots,A_{n})+(A_{1},\ldots,A_{n})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A_{1},\ldots,A_{n})
is given by
\nabla_{i,j}=0
if
j\neq i
or
n+i
, and
\nabla_{i,i}=1_{A_{i}}
and
\nabla_{i,n+i}=1_{A_{i}}
. Then it straightforward to compute that
\left(\langle F\rangle\nabla\right)_{i,j}=0
if
i\neq j
and
\left(\langle F\rangle\nabla\right)_{i,i}=\bigsqcup\limits^{m}_{k=1}\overline{F_{i,k}}\sqcup\bigsqcup\limits^{p}_{k^{\prime}=1}\overline{F_{i,m+k^{\prime}}}=\bigsqcup\limits^{m+p}_{k=1}\overline{F_{i,k}}=\overline{F}_{i,i}
. So we get that
\langle F\rangle\nabla=\overline{F}
.

[D.2]

Abusing notation slightly, since
F
is matrix, we may already write
F
as a block matrix
F=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}F_{1}&F_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right]
where
F_{1}:(A_{1},\ldots,A_{n})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(B_{1},\ldots,B_{m})
is an
n\times m
matrix given by
{F_{1}}_{i,j}=F_{i,j}
, and
F_{2}:(A_{1},\ldots,A_{n})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(C_{1},\ldots,C_{p})
is an
n\times p
matrix given by
{F_{2}}_{i,j}=F_{i,m+j}
. Then it follows that
\langle F\rangle_{i,i}=\overline{F_{1}}_{i,i}
and
\langle F\rangle_{i,n+i}=\overline{F_{2}}_{i,i}
. As such,
\langle F\rangle
can be given as the block matrix
\langle F\rangle=\begin{bmatrix}\overline{F_{1}}&\overline{F_{2}}\end{bmatrix}
. Moreover, we also get that
\overline{F_{1}}F=\begin{bmatrix}F_{1}&0\end{bmatrix}
and
\overline{F_{2}}F=\begin{bmatrix}0&F_{2}\end{bmatrix}
. Similarly, we may also write
F+F
as the block matrix
F+F=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}F&0\\
0&F\end{smallmatrix}\right]
. Then by matrix multiplication, we get that:

\langle F\rangle(F+F)=\begin{bmatrix}\overline{F_{1}}&\overline{F_{2}}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}F&0\\
0&F\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}F_{1}&0\end{bmatrix}&\begin{bmatrix}0&F_{2}\end{bmatrix}\end{bmatrix}

On the other hand, similarly we may write
\iota_{1}+\iota_{2}=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\iota_{1}&0\\
0&\iota_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right]
, and also that
F_{1}\iota_{1}=\begin{bmatrix}F_{1}&0\end{bmatrix}
and
F_{2}\iota_{2}=\begin{bmatrix}0&F_{2}\end{bmatrix}
. As such, we get that:

F(\iota_{1}+\iota_{2})=\begin{bmatrix}F_{1}&F_{2}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\iota_{1}&0\\
0&\iota_{2}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}F_{1}&0\end{bmatrix}&\begin{bmatrix}0&F_{2}\end{bmatrix}\end{bmatrix}

So get that
\langle F\rangle(F+F)=F(\iota_{1}+\iota_{2})
.

Thus
\langle F\rangle
is indeed the decision of
F
, which allows us to conclude that
\mathsf{MAT}\left[(\mathbb{X},\perp)\right]
is an extensive restriction category.

Since we have an extensive restriction category, by Thm
7.10
we get that
\mathsf{MAT}\left[(\mathbb{X},\perp)\right]
is a
\perp_{d}
-restriction category with all binary
\perp_{d}
-joins. We now show that these binary
\perp_{d}
-joins are in fact strong. To do so, let us first work out the binary
\perp_{d}
-joins, for which we need to consider the separating decision. So let us now prove (
ii
).

So suppose that
F\perp_{d}G
, then there is a separating decision
\langle F|G\rangle
. Since
\langle F|G\rangle
is a separating decision, we get that
\langle F|G\rangle_{i,i}=\overline{F}_{i,i}
and
\langle F|G\rangle_{i,n+i}=\overline{G}_{i,i}
. However since each row of
\langle F|G\rangle
is
\perp
-disjoint, we get that
\overline{F}_{i,i}\perp\overline{G}_{i,i}
. Then by definition of
\overline{F}_{i,i}
and
\overline{G}_{i,i}
, from Lemma
4.3
.(
iv
), it follows that
\overline{F_{i,j}}\perp\overline{G_{i,k}}
, and then by Lemma
3.2
we get
F_{i,j}\perp G_{i,k}
. Conversely, if
F_{i,j}\perp G_{i,k}
for all
i,j
and
k
, then we can build the separating decision
\langle F|G\rangle
as
\langle F|G\rangle_{i,j}
if
j\neq i
or
n+i
and
\langle F|G\rangle_{i,i}=\overline{F}_{i,i}
and
\langle F|G\rangle_{i,n+i}=\overline{G}_{i,i}
. By similarly arguments as above, it follows that
\langle F|G\rangle
is a separating decision, so
F\perp_{d}G
. Moreover, it may be worth noting that as a block matrix, we get
\langle F|G\rangle=\begin{bmatrix}\overline{F}&\overline{G}\end{bmatrix}
. Now suppose that
F
and
G
are parallel
\perp_{d}
-disjoint maps. Then since
F_{i,j}\perp G_{i,j}
for all
i
and
j
, we can take their
\perp
-join
F_{i,j}\sqcup G_{i,j}
. Then using (
15
),
F\sqcup G=\langle F|G\rangle(F+G)\nabla
, or by the using the same arguments as in the proof of
[
21
, Prop 9.9]
, we get that
(F\sqcup G)_{i,j}=F_{i,j}\sqcup G_{i,j}
. Now also suppose that
H\perp_{d}F
and
G\perp_{d}F
, which implies that
H_{i,j}\perp F_{i,j}
and
H_{i,j}\perp G_{i,j}
. Then by
[
\boldsymbol{\sqcup}
.4]
, we get that
H_{i,j}\perp F_{i,j}\sqcup G_{i,j}
, so therefore by (
ii
) we have that
H\perp_{d}F\sqcup G
. Thus binary
\perp_{d}
-joins strong in
\mathsf{MAT}\left[(\mathbb{X},\perp)\right]
, and so by Lemma
4.5
,
\mathsf{MAT}\left[(\mathbb{X},\perp)\right]
is a finitely disjoint
\perp_{d}
-restriction category. Moreover, it then follows that
\bigsqcup\limits^{n}_{k=1}F_{k}=\left[\bigsqcup\limits^{n}_{k=1}{F_{k}}_{i,j}\right]_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq n\\
1\leq j\leq m\end{subarray}}
as desired.

Lastly, by the same arguments as in the proof of
[
21
, Prop 9.10]
, we also get that
\mathcal{I}
preserve the disjoint interference restriction structure.

\Box

If we apply this to the maximal interference relation
\perp_{0}
, we get precisely the matrix construction from
[
21
, Sec 9]
. Moreover, by Cor
7.16
, we get that the induced interference relation on the matrix construction is precisely the maximal one as well.

Corollary 8.3

Let
\mathbb{X}
be a restriction category with restriction zeroes. Then
\mathsf{MAT}\left[(\mathbb{X},\perp_{0})\right]
is an extensive restriction category and moreover its separating decision
\perp_{d}
coincides with
\perp_{0}
.

Lastly, we also note that if the base category has all disjoint joins, then so does the matrix category.

Lemma 8.4

For a disjoint
\perp
-restriction category
\mathbb{X}
,
\mathsf{MAT}\left[(\mathbb{X},\perp)\right]
is a disjoint
\perp_{d}
-category, where if
\{F_{k}|~k\in K\}
is a family of pairwise
\perp_{d}
-disjoint parallel
n\times m
matrices, then
\bigsqcup\limits_{k\in I}F_{k}=\left[\bigsqcup\limits_{k\in K}{F_{k}}_{i,j}\right]_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq n\\
1\leq j\leq m\end{subarray}}
.

Proof:
This follows by a similar arguments to the finite disjoint join case above.

\Box

9
Iteration and Trace

In this section we show that for an extensive restriction category, to give a trace operator is equivalent to giving a Kleene wand, which is one of the main results of this paper. We begin by first reviewing trace operators on coproducts, and how to give a trace operator is equivalent to giving a
parametrized iteration operator
. This perspective will be very useful since, as we will see below, for an extensive restriction category, a parametrized iteration operator is essentially a Kleene wand.

Briefly recall that a
traced symmetric monoidal category
is a symmetric
3
3

3

It is worth noting that in
[
34
]
, Joyal, Street, and Verity first introduced traced monoidal categories as
braided
monoidal categories equipped with a trace operator. However since we are dealing with coproducts, we are working in the symmetric setting.
monoidal category with monoidal product with a trace operator. Since every category with finite coproducts is a symmetric monoidal category
[
37
, Sec 6.2]
, where the monoidal product is the coproduct and the monoidal unit is the initial object, one can indeed consider a trace operator for coproducts. As such, the following definition below of a trace operator is the same as for a symmetric monoidal category, but where we have already specified our monoidal structure to be that given by coproducts. There are many equivalent lists of axioms for a trace operator on symmetric monoidal category. Here, we will use the (strict version) of the one presented in
[
32
, Def 2.2]
. For other equivalent axiomatizations of the trace operator, see for example
[
27
, Def 4.1]
or
[
29
, Def 2.1]
or
[
31
, Sec 3]
. For a more in-depth introduction to traced symmetric monoidal categories, including their graphical calculus, we invite the reader to see
[
3
,
24
,
27
,
31
,
29
,
30
,
32
,
37
,
34
]
.

Definition 9.1

A
traced coCartesian monoidal category

[
37
, Sec 6.4]
is a category
\mathbb{X}
with finite coproducts which comes equipped with a
trace operator

\mathsf{Tr}

[
32
, Def 2.2]
which is a family of operators (indexed by triples of objects
X,A,B\in\mathbb{X}
):

\displaystyle\mathsf{Tr}^{X}_{A,B}:\mathbb{X}(X+A,X+B)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{X}(A,B)

\displaystyle\mathsf{Tr}^{X}_{A,B}(f):A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Bf:X+A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X+B

such that the following axioms hold:

[Tightening]:

For every map
f:X+A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X+B
,
g:A^{\prime}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
, and
h:B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B^{\prime}
the following equality holds:

\begin{gathered}\mathsf{Tr}^{X}_{A^{\prime},B^{\prime}}\left((1_{X}+g)f(1_{X}+h)\right)=g\mathsf{Tr}^{X}_{A,B}(f)h\end{gathered}

(21)

[Sliding]:

For every map
f:X+A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X^{\prime}+B
and
k:X^{\prime}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X
, the following equality holds:

\begin{gathered}\mathsf{Tr}^{X}_{A,B}\left(f(k+1_{B})\right)=\mathsf{Tr}^{X^{\prime}}_{A,B}\left((k+1_{A})f\right)\end{gathered}

(22)

[Vanishing]:

For every map
f:X+Y+A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X+Y+B
, the following equality holds:

\begin{gathered}\mathsf{Tr}^{X+Y}_{A,B}(f)=\mathsf{Tr}^{Y}_{A,B}\left(\mathsf{Tr}^{X}_{Y+A,Y+B}\left(f\right)\right)\end{gathered}

(23)

[Superposing]:

For every map
f:X+A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X+B
and every map
g:C\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}D
the following equality holds:

\begin{gathered}\mathsf{Tr}^{X}_{A+C,B+D}\left(f+g\right)=\mathsf{Tr}^{X}_{A,B}(f)+g\end{gathered}

(24)

[Yanking]:

For every object
X
, the following equality holds:

\begin{gathered}\mathsf{Tr}^{X}_{X,X}(\sigma)=1_{X}\end{gathered}

(25)

where recall that
\sigma:X+X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X+X
is the canonical symmetry isomorphism for the coproduct.

For a map
f:X+A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X+B
, the map
\mathsf{Tr}^{X}_{A,B}(f):A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
is called the
trace
of
f
.

In
[
1
]
, Abramsky referred to trace operators on coproducts as “particle style traces”, as the trace can intuitively be interpreted by streams of particles or tokens flowing around a network – see also
[
3
, Sec 1.1]
and
[
27
, Sec 4.1]
for more explination about this intuition. Another desirable property one might ask of the trace operator is that it be
uniform
.

Definition 9.2

For a traced coCartesian monoidal category
\mathbb{X}
, its trace operator
\mathsf{Tr}
is said to be
uniform

[
30
, Def 2.2]
if the following holds:

[Uniform]:

For all
f:X+A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X+B
,
f^{\prime}:Y+A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y+B
, and
h:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
such that
f(h+1_{B})=(h+1_{A})f^{\prime}
, then
\mathsf{Tr}^{X}_{A,B}(f)=\mathsf{Tr}^{Y}_{A,B}(f^{\prime})
.

As explained by Selinger in
[
36
, Sec 3.2]
, uniformity is a proof principle for showing that two traces are equal. We note that often times uniformity is only with respect to a certain subclass of maps, called
strict
maps
[
30
, Def 2.1]
. However in this paper, we consider uniformity to hold for all maps, or in other words, that all maps are strict.

As mentioned above, trace operators on coproducts admit an alternative characterization via another kind of operator. Indeed, consider a map of type
f:X+A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X+B
. By the couniversal property of the coproduct, this is actually a copairing of maps
f_{1}:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X+B
and
f_{2}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X+B
, so
f=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}f_{1}\\
f_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right]
. Using
[Tightening]
and
[Sliding]
, it turns out that the following equality holds:

\mathsf{Tr}^{X}_{A,B}(f)=f_{2}\begin{bmatrix}\mathsf{Tr}^{X}_{X,B}(\nabla f_{1})\\
1_{B}\end{bmatrix}

where note that
\mathsf{Tr}^{X}_{X,B}(\nabla f_{1}):X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
. Therefore, we see that the traced out part of a map of type
X+A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X+B
is essentially determined by its
X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X+B
component. As such, this gives rise to an operator which takes in a map of type
X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X+B
and produces a map of type
X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
, called a
parametrized iteration operation
.

Definition 9.3

For a category
\mathbb{X}
with finite coproducts, a
parametrized iteration operator

\mathsf{Iter}

[
37
, Prop 6.8]
is a family of operators (indexed by pairs of objects
X,A\in\mathbb{X}
):

\displaystyle\mathsf{Iter}^{X}_{A}:\mathbb{X}(X,X+A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{X}(X,A)

\displaystyle\mathsf{Iter}^{X}_{A}(f):X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Af:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X+A

such that the following axioms hold:

[Parametrized Iteration]

For every map
