f:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X+A
the following equality holds:

\begin{gathered}f\begin{bmatrix}\mathsf{Iter}^{X}_{A}(f)\\
1_{A}\end{bmatrix}=\mathsf{Iter}^{X}_{A}(f)\end{gathered}

(26)

[Naturality]

For every map
f:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X+A^{\prime}
and every map
h:A^{\prime}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
the following equality holds:

\begin{gathered}\mathsf{Iter}^{X}_{A}\left(f(1_{X}+h)\right)=\mathsf{Iter}^{X}_{A^{\prime}}\left(f\right)h\end{gathered}

(27)

[Dinaturality]

For every map
f:X^{\prime}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X+A
and every map
k:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X^{\prime}
the following equality holds:

\begin{gathered}\mathsf{Iter}^{X}_{A}\left(kf\right)=k\mathsf{Iter}^{X^{\prime}}_{A}\left(f(k+1_{A})\right)\end{gathered}

(28)

[Diagonal Property]

For every map
f:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X+X+A
the following equality holds:

\begin{gathered}\mathsf{Iter}^{X}_{A}\left(\mathsf{Iter}^{X}_{X+A}(f)\right)=\mathsf{Iter}^{X}_{A}\left(f(\nabla_{X}+1_{A})\right)\end{gathered}

(29)

For a map
f:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X+A
, the map
\mathsf{Iter}^{X}_{A}(f):X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is called the
iteration
of
f
.

As the name suggests, a parametrized iteration operator can intuitively be interpreted as iterating the map
{f:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X+A}
if it continues to land in
X
and only stopping when it lands in
A
. This intuition is best captured by our main example of partial functions given below. As with trace operators, a desirable property for a parametrized iteration operator is to be
uniform
.

Definition 9.4

For a category
\mathbb{X}
with finite coproducts, a parametrized iteration operator
\mathsf{Iter}
is said to be
uniform

[
30
, Sec 4.3]
if the following holds:

[Uniform]:

For all
f:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X+A
,
f^{\prime}:Y\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y+A
, and
h:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
such that
f(h+1_{A})=hf^{\prime}
, then
\mathsf{Iter}^{X}_{A}(f)=h\mathsf{Iter}^{Y}_{A}(f^{\prime})
.

We now review how having a trace operator is equivalent to having a parametrized iteration operator and, in particular, how to build one from the other.

Proposition 9.5

[
37
, Prop 6.8]
For a category with finite coproducts, there is a bijective correspondence between (uniform) trace operators and (uniform) parametrized iteration operators. Explicitly:

(i)

Let
\mathsf{Iter}
be a (uniform) parametrized iteration operator, then, for a map
f:X+A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X+B
, define its trace
{\mathsf{Tr}^{X}_{A,B}(f):A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B}
as

\displaystyle\mathsf{Tr}^{X}_{A,B}(f):=\iota_{2}f\begin{bmatrix}\mathsf{Iter}^{X}_{B}(\iota_{1}f)\\
1_{B}\end{bmatrix}

(30)

then
\mathsf{Tr}
is a (uniform) trace operator.

(ii)

Let
\mathsf{Tr}
be a (uniform) trace operator, then, for a map
f:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X+B
, define its iteration
\mathsf{Iter}^{X}_{B}(f):X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
as

\displaystyle\mathsf{Iter}^{X}_{B}(f):=\mathsf{Tr}^{X}_{X,B}\left(\begin{bmatrix}f\\
f\end{bmatrix}\right)=\mathsf{Tr}^{X}_{X,B}\left(\nabla_{X}f\right)

(31)

then
\mathsf{Iter}
is a (uniform) parametrized iteration operator.

Furthermore, these constructions are inverses of each other. In other words, a coCartesian traced monoidal category is precisely a category finite coproducts equipped with a parametrized iteration operator.

Our main example of a coCartesian traced monoidal category is the category of sets and partial functions. Other examples of coCartesian traced monoidal categories can be found in
[
27
, Ex 4.6]
. In particular, every partially additive category is a coCartesian traced monoidal category
[
24
, Prop 3.1.4]
.

Example 9.6

\mathsf{PAR}
is a traced coCartesian monoidal category where the trace operator and parametrized iteration operator are induced by the natural feedback operator. So for a partial function
f:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X\sqcup Y
, its iteration
{\mathsf{Iter}^{X}_{Y}(f):X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y}
is the partial function defined as follows:

\displaystyle\mathsf{Iter}^{X}_{Y}(f)(x)=\begin{cases}y&\text{ if }f(x)=(y,2)\\
y&\text{ if }\exists~n\in\mathbb{N},n\geq 1~\exists~x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in X\text{ s.t. }f(x)=(x_{0},1)\\
&\text{ and }f(x_{0})=(x_{1},1)\text{ and }\ldots\text{ and }f(x_{n})=(y,2)\\
\uparrow&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

For a partial function
g:X\sqcup Y\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X\sqcup Z
, its trace
\mathsf{Tr}^{X}_{Y,Z}(g):Y\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Z
is the partial function defined as follows:

\displaystyle\mathsf{Tr}^{X}_{Y,Z}(g)(y)=\begin{cases}z&\text{ if }g(y,2)=(z,2)\\
z&\text{ if }\exists~n\in\mathbb{N},n\geq 1~\exists~x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in X\text{ s.t. }g(y,2)=(x_{0},1)\\
&\text{ and }g(x_{0},1)=(x_{1},1)\text{ and }\ldots\text{ and }g(x_{n},1)=(z,2)\\
\uparrow&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

Furthermore,
\mathsf{Iter}
and
\mathsf{Tr}
are uniform.

Since restriction coproducts are actual coproducts, one can consider trace operators and parametrized iteration operators for restriction coproducts. In particular, we are interested in these operators for extensive restriction categories.

Definition 9.7

A
traced extensive restriction category
is an extensive restriction category equipped with a trace operator or equivalently a parametrized iteration operator.

Our objective is to show that for an extensive restriction category, to give a parametrized iteration operator is equivalent to giving a Kleene wand. To do so, let us first revisit the axioms of a parametrized iteration operator using the matrix calculus for extensive restriction categories. So suppose we are in a traced extensive restriction category with parametrized iteration operator
\mathsf{Iter}
. First observe that in an extensive restriction category, to give a map of type
f:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X+A
is equivalent to giving maps
f_{1}:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X
and
f_{2}:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
such that
f_{1}\perp_{d}f_{2}
, since
f
is in fact a
1\times 2
matrix
f=\begin{bmatrix}f_{1}&f_{2}\end{bmatrix}
. So the parametrized iteration operator is really acting on an endomorphism and a map with the same domain that are
\perp_{d}
-disjoint maps. So we may write:

\mathsf{Iter}^{X}_{A}\left(\begin{bmatrix}f_{1}&f_{2}\end{bmatrix}\right):X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Af:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X~~~g:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A~~~f\perp_{d}g

So we see that a parametrized iteration operator and a Kleene wand are operators with the same inputs and outputs. This is our first hint that in this setting, a parametrized iteration operator and a Kleene wand are in fact the same thing. It is important to note that in
[
13
, Sec 4]
, they were working in a distributive extensive restriction category with joins, which by Cor
7.16
implies that
\perp_{0}=\perp_{d}
. The objective here however is to show that we still get the desired correspondence for arbitrary extensive restriction categories when taking Kleene wands on
\perp_{d}
instead (since in an arbitrary extensive restriction category,
\perp_{d}
may not equal
\perp_{0}
).

Now let us consider re-expressing the axioms of a parametrized iteration operator using the matrix calculus. To avoid heavy notation, let us introduce the following notation for our parametrized iteration operator:

\displaystyle\mathsf{Iter}^{X}_{A}\left(\begin{bmatrix}f_{1}&f_{2}\end{bmatrix}\right)=\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}&f_{2}\end{array}\!\!\!\right]

Now starting with
[Parametrized Iteration]
, first note that by using matrix multiplication we have that:

\begin{bmatrix}f_{1}&f_{2}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}&f_{2}\end{array}\!\!\!\right]\\
1_{A}\end{bmatrix}=f_{1}\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}&f_{2}\end{array}\!\!\!\right]\sqcup f_{2}

Thus we may re-express the iteration axiom as follows (where we slightly modify the above equality using commutativity of
\sqcup
):

[Parametrized Iteration]

For every map
f_{1}:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X
and
f_{2}:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
such that
f_{1}\perp f_{2}
, the following equality holds:

\begin{gathered}f_{2}\sqcup f_{1}\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}&f_{2}\end{array}\!\!\!\right]=\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}&f_{2}\end{array}\!\!\!\right]\end{gathered}

(33)

Next, for
[Naturality]
and
[Dinaturality]
, first note that
1_{X}+h:X+A^{\prime}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X+A
and
k+1_{A}:X+A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X^{\prime}+A
are respectively the following diagonal square matrices:

\displaystyle 1_{X}+h=\begin{bmatrix}1_{X}&0\\
0&h\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle k+1_{A}=\begin{bmatrix}k&0\\
0&1_{A}\end{bmatrix}

Moreover, by matrix multiplication, it is easy to compute that we have the following equalities:

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}f_{1}&f_{2}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1_{X}&0\\
0&h\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}f_{1}&f_{2}h\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle k\begin{bmatrix}f_{1}&f_{2}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}kf_{1}&kf_{2}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}f_{1}&f_{2}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}k&0\\
0&1_{A}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}f_{1}k&f_{2}\end{bmatrix}

Thus we may re-express the naturality and dinaturality axioms as follows:

[Naturality]

For every map
f_{1}:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X
and
f_{2}:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A^{\prime}
such that
f_{1}\perp f_{2}
, and every map
h:A^{\prime}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
, the following equality holds:

\begin{gathered}\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}&f_{2}h\end{array}\!\!\!\right]=\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}&f_{2}\end{array}\!\!\!\right]h\end{gathered}

(34)

[Dinaturality]

For every map
f_{1}:X^{\prime}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X
and
f_{2}:X^{\prime}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
such that
f_{1}\perp f_{2}
, and every map
k:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X^{\prime}
, the following equality holds:

\begin{gathered}\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}kf_{1}&kf_{2}\end{array}\!\!\!\right]=k\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}k&f_{2}\end{array}\!\!\!\right]\end{gathered}

(35)

Then for the last axiom
[Diagonal Property]
, first note that a map
f:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X+X+A
is equivalently given by three maps
\{f_{1}:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X,f_{2}:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X,f_{3}:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A\}
which are decision disjoint. So
f
is in fact a
1\times 3
row matrix,
f=\begin{bmatrix}f_{1}&f_{2}&f_{3}\end{bmatrix}
. Next observe that
\nabla_{X}+1_{A}
is the
3\times 2
matrix:

\nabla_{X}+1_{A}=\begin{bmatrix}1_{X}&0\\
1_{X}&0\\
0&1_{A}\end{bmatrix}

and so we get that:

\begin{bmatrix}f_{1}&f_{2}&f_{3}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1_{X}&0\\
1_{X}&0\\
0&1_{A}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}f_{1}\sqcup f_{2}&f_{3}\end{bmatrix}

Next note that
\begin{bmatrix}f_{1}&f_{2}&f_{3}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}f_{1}&\begin{bmatrix}f_{2}&f_{3}\end{bmatrix}\end{bmatrix}
. So we may take its iteration, which we denote by
\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|cc}f_{1}&f_{2}&f_{3}\end{array}\!\!\!\right]:=\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}&\begin{bmatrix}f_{2}&f_{3}\end{bmatrix}\end{array}\!\!\!\right]:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X+A
. Note that
\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|cc}f_{1}&f_{2}&f_{3}\end{array}\!\!\!\right]
is a
1\times 2
row matrix, and thus fully determined by post-composing it with the quasi-projections
\iota^{\circ}_{1}
and
\iota^{\circ}_{2}
. Then by
[Naturality]
, it follows that
\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}&\begin{bmatrix}f_{2}&f_{3}\end{bmatrix}\end{array}\!\!\!\right]\iota^{\circ}_{1}=\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}&f_{2}\end{array}\!\!\!\right]
, and similarly
\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}&\begin{bmatrix}f_{2}&f_{3}\end{bmatrix}\end{array}\!\!\!\right]\iota^{\circ}_{2}=\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}&f_{3}\end{array}\!\!\!\right]
. Therefore, we have that:

\displaystyle\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}&\begin{bmatrix}f_{2}&f_{3}\end{bmatrix}\end{array}\!\!\!\right]=\begin{bmatrix}\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}&f_{2}\end{array}\!\!\!\right]&\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}&f_{3}\end{array}\!\!\!\right]\end{bmatrix}

So we may re-express the diagonal axiom as follows:

[Diagonal Property]

For every
\{f_{1}:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X,f_{2}:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X,f_{3}:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A\}
which are decision disjoint, the following equality holds:

\begin{gathered}\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}&f_{2}\end{array}\!\!\!\right]&\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}&f_{3}\end{array}\!\!\!\right]\end{array}\!\!\!\right]=\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}\sqcup f_{2}&f_{3}\end{array}\!\!\!\right]\end{gathered}

(37)

We can also re-express uniformity as follows:

[Uniform]:

For every map
f_{1}:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X
and
f_{2}:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
such that
f_{1}\perp_{d}f_{2}
, and
f^{\prime}_{1}:Y\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
and
f^{\prime}_{2}:Y\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
such that
f^{\prime}_{1}\perp f^{\prime}_{2}
, and
h:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
such that
hf^{\prime}_{1}=f_{1}h
and
f_{2}=hf^{\prime}_{2}
, then the following equality holds:

\displaystyle\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}&f_{2}\end{array}\!\!\!\right]=h\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f^{\prime}_{1}&f^{\prime}_{2}\end{array}\!\!\!\right]

To summarize, we have that:

Lemma 9.8

For an extensive restriction category,
\mathsf{Iter}
is a (uniform) parametrized iteration operator if and only if
\mathsf{Iter}
satisfies (
33
), (
34
), (
35
), and (
37
) (and (
[Uniform]:
)).

Now let us consider the induced trace operator. A map
f:X+A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X+B
is in fact a
2\times 2
square matrix
f=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}f_{1}&f_{2}\\
f_{3}&f_{4}\end{smallmatrix}\right]
, where
f_{1}:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X
,
f_{2}:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
,
f_{3}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X
, and
f_{4}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
such that
f_{1}\perp_{d}f_{2}
and
f_{3}\perp_{d}f_{4}
. As such, our induced trace operator
\mathsf{Tr}
acts on
2\times 2
square matrices. Inspired by our notation for our parametrized iteration operator, we use the following notation to denote our traces:

\displaystyle\mathsf{Tr}^{X}_{A,B}\left(\begin{bmatrix}f_{1}&f_{2}\\
f_{3}&f_{4}\end{bmatrix}\right)=\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}&f_{2}\\
\hline\cr f_{3}&f_{4}\end{array}\right]

Now note that we have that
\iota_{1}f=\begin{bmatrix}f_{1}&f_{2}\end{bmatrix}
and
\iota_{2}f=\begin{bmatrix}f_{3}&f_{4}\end{bmatrix}
. Therefore, the trace is given as follows:

\displaystyle\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}&f_{2}\\
\hline\cr f_{3}&f_{4}\end{array}\right]=f_{4}\sqcup f_{3}\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}&f_{2}\end{array}\!\!\!\right]

and the trace axioms can be re-expressed in terms of square matrices as follows:

[Tightening]:

\begin{gathered}\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}&f_{2}h\\
\hline\cr gf_{3}&gf_{4}h\end{array}\right]=g\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}&f_{2}\\
\hline\cr f_{3}&f_{4}\end{array}\right]h\end{gathered}

(45)

[Sliding]:

\begin{gathered}\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}k&f_{2}\\
\hline\cr f_{3}k&f_{4}\end{array}\right]=\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}kf_{1}&kf_{2}\\
\hline\cr f_{3}&f_{4}\end{array}\right]\end{gathered}

(46)

[Vanishing]:

\begin{gathered}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc|c}f_{1}&f_{2}&f_{3}\\
f_{4}&f_{5}&f_{6}\\
\hline\cr f_{7}&f_{8}&f_{9}\end{array}\right]=\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}&f_{2}\\
\hline\cr f_{4}&f_{5}\end{array}\right]&\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}&f_{3}\\
\hline\cr f_{4}&f_{6}\end{array}\right]\\
\\
\hline\cr\\
\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}&f_{2}\\
\hline\cr f_{7}&f_{8}\end{array}\right]&\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}&f_{3}\\
\hline\cr f_{7}&f_{9}\end{array}\right]\end{array}\right]\end{gathered}

(47)

[Superposing]:

\begin{gathered}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc|c}f_{1}&f_{2}&0\\
f_{3}&f_{4}&0\\
\hline\cr 0&0&g\end{array}\right]=\begin{bmatrix}\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}&f_{2}\\
\hline\cr f_{3}&f_{4}\end{array}\right]&0\\
0&g\end{bmatrix}\end{gathered}

(48)

[Yanking]:

\begin{gathered}\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}0&1_{X}\\
\hline\cr 1_{X}&0\end{array}\right]=1_{X}\end{gathered}

(49)

[Uniform]:

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}f_{1}h&f_{2}\\
f_{3}h&f_{4}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}hf^{\prime}_{1}&hf^{\prime}_{2}\\
f^{\prime}_{3}&f^{\prime}_{4}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow

\displaystyle\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}&f_{2}\\
\hline\cr f_{3}&f_{4}\end{array}\right]=\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}f^{\prime}_{1}&f^{\prime}_{2}\\
\hline\cr f^{\prime}_{3}&f^{\prime}_{4}\end{array}\right]

On the other hand, we can recapture the parametrized iteration operator from the trace as follows:

\displaystyle\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}&f_{2}\end{array}\!\!\!\right]=\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}&f_{2}\\
\hline\cr f_{1}&f_{2}\end{array}\right]

We are now finally ready to show the equivalence between parametrized iteration operators and Kleene wands. We begin by showing how a Kleene wand gives a parametrized iteration operator, and thus also a trace operator.

Proposition 9.9

Let
\mathbb{X}
be an extensive restriction category which comes equipped with a Kleene wand
\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}
for
\perp_{d}
– that is,
(\mathbb{X},\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}})
is a
\perp_{d}
-itegory – then
\mathbb{X}
has a parametrized iteration operator
\mathsf{Iter}
defined as follows on a map
{f=\begin{bmatrix}f_{1}&f_{2}\end{bmatrix}:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X+A}
:

\displaystyle\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}&f_{2}\end{array}\!\!\!\right]=f_{1}\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}f_{2}

and the induced trace operator
\mathsf{Tr}
is defined as follows on a map
g=\begin{bmatrix}g_{1}&g_{2}\\
g_{3}&g_{4}\end{bmatrix}:X+A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X+B
:

\displaystyle\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}g_{1}&g_{2}\\
\hline\cr g_{3}&g_{4}\end{array}\right]=g_{4}\sqcup g_{3}(g_{1}\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}g_{2})

So
\mathbb{X}
is a traced extensive restriction category. Moreover, if
\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}
is uniform, then
\mathsf{Iter}
and
\mathsf{Tr}
are uniform.

Proof:
First note that our parametrized iteration operator is indeed well-defined since given a map
{f:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X+A}
, there are unique maps
f_{1}:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X
and
f_{2}:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
with
f_{1}\perp_{d}f_{2}
such that
f=\begin{bmatrix}f_{1}&f_{2}\end{bmatrix}
. So
f_{1}\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}f_{2}
is indeed well-defined. We now show that
\mathsf{Iter}
satisfies the four axioms of a parametrized iteration operator. By Lemma
9.8
, we can prove their equivalent versions in an extensive restriction category.

[Parametrized Iteration]

Using
[
\boldsymbol{\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-4.0pt\cr$|$\cr}}}}
.1]
, we compute that:

f_{2}\sqcup f_{1}\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}&f_{2}\end{array}\!\!\!\right]=f_{2}\sqcup f_{1}\left(f_{1}\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}f_{2}\right)=f_{1}\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}f_{2}=\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}&f_{2}\end{array}\!\!\!\right]

[Naturality]

Using
[
\boldsymbol{\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-4.0pt\cr$|$\cr}}}}
.2]
, we compute that:

\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}&f_{2}h\end{array}\!\!\!\right]=f_{1}\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}f_{2}h=(f_{1}\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}f_{2})h=\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}&f_{2}\end{array}\!\!\!\right]h

[Dinaturality]

Using
[
\boldsymbol{\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-4.0pt\cr$|$\cr}}}}
.3]
, we compute that:

\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}kf_{1}&kf_{2}\end{array}\!\!\!\right]=kf_{1}\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}kf_{2}=k(f_{1}k\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}f_{2})=k\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}k&f_{2}\end{array}\!\!\!\right]

[Diagonal Property]

Using
[
\boldsymbol{\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-4.0pt\cr$|$\cr}}}}
.4]
, we compute that:

\displaystyle\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}\sqcup f_{2}&f_{3}\end{array}\!\!\!\right]=(f_{1}\sqcup f_{2})\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}f_{3}=(f_{1}\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}f_{2})\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}(f_{1}\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}f_{3})=\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}f_{2}&f_{1}\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}f_{3}\end{array}\!\!\!\right]=\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}&f_{2}\end{array}\!\!\!\right]&\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}&f_{3}\end{array}\!\!\!\right]\end{array}\!\!\!\right]

Thus
\mathsf{Iter}
is a parametrized iteration operator as desired. Moreover, from (
9
), we get that (
9.9
) is indeed the formula for the induced trace operator
\mathsf{Tr}
. Lastly, suppose that
\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}
is also uniform. Then we show that
\mathsf{Iter}
is also uniform:

[Uniform]

Suppose that
f_{1}\perp_{d}f_{2}
and
f_{1}^{\prime}\perp_{d}f^{\prime}_{2}
, and that we have a map
h
such that
hf^{\prime}_{1}=f_{1}h
and
f_{2}=hf^{\prime}_{2}
. Since
\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}
is uniform, we get that
h(f^{\prime}_{1}\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}f^{\prime}_{2})=f_{1}\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}f_{2}
, or in other words,
h\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f^{\prime}_{1}&f^{\prime}_{2}\end{array}\!\!\!\right]=\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}&f_{2}\end{array}\!\!\!\right]
.

Since
\mathsf{Iter}
is uniform, we also get that
\mathsf{Tr}
is uniform.

\Box

Conversely, we now show how a parametrized iteration operator gives a Kleene wand.

Proposition 9.10

Let
\mathbb{X}
be a traced extensive restriction category with parametrized iteration operator,
\mathsf{Iter}
, and an induced trace operator
\mathsf{Tr}
, then
(\mathbb{X},\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}})
is a
\perp_{d}
-itegory, where the Kleene wand
\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}
is defined for
\perp_{d}
-disjoint maps
f:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X
and
g:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
as follows:

\displaystyle f\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}g:=\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f&g\end{array}\!\!\!\right]=\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}f&g\\
\hline\cr f&g\end{array}\right]

Thus,
(\mathbb{X},\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}})
is a
\perp_{d}
-itegory. Moreover, if
\mathsf{Iter}
is uniform, then
\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}
is uniform.

Proof:
Since
f\perp_{d}g
, we can indeed build the
1\times 2
matrix
\begin{bmatrix}f&g\end{bmatrix}:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X+A
. So we can take its iteration, and therefore our proposed Kleene wand is well-defined. We now need to prove the four axioms of a Kleene wand.

[
\boldsymbol{\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-4.44444pt\cr$|$\cr}}}}
.1]

Here we use
[Parametrized Iteration]
to compute that:

g\sqcup f(f\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}g)=g\sqcup f\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f&g\end{array}\!\!\!\right]=\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f&g\end{array}\!\!\!\right]=f\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}g

[
\boldsymbol{\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-4.44444pt\cr$|$\cr}}}}
.2]

Here we use
[Naturality]
to compute that:

\left(f\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}g\right)h=\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f&g\end{array}\!\!\!\right]h=\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f&gh\end{array}\!\!\!\right]=f\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}gh

[
\boldsymbol{\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-4.44444pt\cr$|$\cr}}}}
.3]

Here we use
[Dinaturality]
to compute that:

hf\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}hg=\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}hf&hg\end{array}\!\!\!\right]=h\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}hf&g\end{array}\!\!\!\right]=h\left(fh\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}g\right)

[
\boldsymbol{\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-4.44444pt\cr$|$\cr}}}}
.4]

If
f\sqcup f^{\prime}\perp_{d}g
, we will first show that
\{f,f^{\prime},g\}
are decision disjoint. So define
\langle f|f^{\prime}|g\rangle=\langle f\sqcup f^{\prime}|g\rangle(\langle f|f^{\prime}\rangle+1_{X})
. To show that this is the separating decision of
\{f,f^{\prime},g\}
, by Lemma
7.12
.(
i
), we need to show that is the restriction inverse of
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\overline{f}\\
\overline{f^{\prime}}\\
\overline{g}\end{smallmatrix}\right]
. However recall that by Lemma
7.9
.(
i
),
\langle f\sqcup f^{\prime}|g\rangle
is the restriction inverse of
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\overline{f\sqcup f^{\prime}}\\
\overline{g}\end{smallmatrix}\right]
and
\langle f|f^{\prime}\rangle
is the restriction inverse of
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\overline{f}\\
\overline{f^{\prime}}\end{smallmatrix}\right]
. So by construction,
\langle f|f^{\prime}|g\rangle
is the restriction inverse of
\left(\begin{bmatrix}\overline{f}\\
\overline{f^{\prime}}\end{bmatrix}+1_{X}\right)\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\overline{f\sqcup f^{\prime}}\\
\overline{g}\end{smallmatrix}\right]
, since it is the composition and coproduct of restriction isomorphisms. However observe that by
[R.3]
and Lemma
4.3
.(
i
), we get that
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\overline{f}\\
\overline{f^{\prime}}\end{smallmatrix}\right]\overline{f\sqcup f^{\prime}}=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\overline{f}\\
\overline{f^{\prime}}\end{smallmatrix}\right]
. Then we have that
\left(\begin{bmatrix}\overline{f}\\
\overline{f^{\prime}}\end{bmatrix}+1_{X}\right)\begin{bmatrix}\overline{f\sqcup f^{\prime}}\\
\overline{g}\end{bmatrix}=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\overline{f}\\
\overline{f^{\prime}}\\
\overline{g}\end{smallmatrix}\right]
. So
\langle f|f^{\prime}|g\rangle
is the restriction inverse of
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\overline{f}\\
\overline{f^{\prime}}\\
\overline{g}\end{smallmatrix}\right]
, and therefore is also the separating decision which make
\{f,f^{\prime},g\}
decision disjoint. Since
\{f,f^{\prime},g\}
are decision disjoint, we can build the row matrix
\begin{bmatrix}f&f^{\prime}&g\end{bmatrix}
. We first need to explain why
f\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}g
and
f\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}f^{\prime}
are
\perp_{d}
-disjoint. However note that from (
9
), we get that
\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f&g\end{array}\!\!\!\right]\perp_{d}\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f&f^{\prime}\end{array}\!\!\!\right]
, or in other words,
f\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}g\perp_{d}f\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}f^{\prime}
as desired. Finally, using
[Diagonal Property]
, we compute that:

\displaystyle(f\sqcup f^{\prime})\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}g=\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f\sqcup f^{\prime}&g\end{array}\!\!\!\right]=\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f&f^{\prime}\end{array}\!\!\!\right]&\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f&g\end{array}\!\!\!\right]\end{array}\!\!\!\right]=\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}f^{\prime}&f\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}g\end{array}\!\!\!\right]=(f\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}f^{\prime})\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}(f\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}g)

So we conclude that
\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}
is a Kleene wand. Now suppose that
\mathsf{Iter}
is also uniform. Given
f\perp_{d}g
and
f^{\prime}\perp_{d}g^{\prime}
, and a map
h
such that
hg^{\prime}=g
and
hf^{\prime}=fh
, then
h\left(f^{\prime}\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}g^{\prime}\right)=f\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}g
. Then by
[Uniform]
we get that
f\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}g=\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f&g\end{array}\!\!\!\right]=h\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f^{\prime}&g^{\prime}\end{array}\!\!\!\right]=h\left(f^{\prime}\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}g^{\prime}\right)
. So
\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}
is also uniform, as desired.

\Box

It is immediate that the constructions in Prop
9.9
and Prop
9.10
are inverses of each other. Thus we obtain the main result of this paper:

Theorem 9.11

For an extensive restriction category, there is a bijective correspondence between parametrized iteration operators (equivalently trace operators) and Kleene wands for
\perp_{d}
. Therefore, a traced extensive restriction category is equivalently an extensive restriction category which is an itegory with respect to
\perp_{d}
.

In the case that we have infinite disjoint joins, we can use the canonical inductive Kleene wand to get a parametrized iteration operator and trace operator.

Proposition 9.12

Let
\mathbb{X}
be an extensive restriction category which is also a disjoint
\perp_{d}
-restriction category, then
\mathbb{X}
is a traced extensive restriction category with parametrized iteration operator
\mathsf{Iter}
and trace operator
\mathsf{Tr}
defined respectively as follows:

\displaystyle\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}&f_{2}\end{array}\!\!\!\right]=\bigsqcup\limits^{\infty}_{n=0}f_{1}^{n}f_{2}

\displaystyle\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}g_{1}&g_{2}\\
\hline\cr g_{3}&g_{4}\end{array}\right]=g_{4}\sqcup\bigsqcup\limits^{\infty}_{n=0}g_{3}g_{1}^{n}g_{2}

Moreover,
\mathsf{Iter}
and
\mathsf{Tr}
are uniform.

Proof:
These are the induced parametrized iteration operator and trace operator from the Kleene wand from Thm
5.16
. Since this an inductive Kleene wand, it is uniform, and therefore the parametrized iteration operator and trace operator are both uniform as well.

\Box

Moreover, we also note that it is not difficult to see that an extensive restriction category which is also a disjoint
\perp_{d}
-restriction category is in fact a partially additive category where the partial sum operation
\Sigma
is given by the
\perp_{d}
-joins
\bigsqcup
. Moreover in this case, the canonical trace formula for a partially additive category as in
[
24
, Prop 3.1.4]
is precisely the one given in (
9.12
) above.

We can also consider the classical setting, where we can use the Kleene upper-star to express the parametrized iteration operator and trace operator.

Proposition 9.13

Let
\mathbb{X}
be an extensive restriction category which is also a classical
\perp_{d}
-restriction category equipped with a Kleene upper-star
\star
, then
\mathbb{X}
is a traced extensive restriction category with a parametrized iteration operator
\mathsf{Iter}
and trace operator
\mathsf{Tr}
defined respectively by:

\displaystyle\left[\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1}&f_{2}\end{array}\!\!\!\right]=f_{1}^{\star}f_{2}

\displaystyle\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}g_{1}&g_{2}\\
\hline\cr g_{3}&g_{4}\end{array}\right]=g_{4}\sqcup g_{3}g_{1}^{\star}g_{2}

Proof:
These are the induced parametrized iteration operator and trace operator from the induced Kleene wand from a Kleene upper-star as given in Prop
6.8
.

\Box

Either of these above formulas recapture the canonical trace and iteration of partial functions.

Example 9.14

For
\mathsf{PAR}
, the parametrized iteration operator and trace operator given in Ex
9.6
are precisely the ones obtain from the Kleene wand in Ex
5.4
. Moreover, they are also given by the formulas in (
9.12
) and (
9.13
).

We conclude by explaining how applying the matrix construction to an itegory gives a traced extensive restriction category. Once one has matrices, it is much easier to use the trace operator rather than the Kleene wand or the parametrized iteration operator. To understand the formula for the trace in the matrix construction, we first need to understand what the trace of an
n+m\times n+p
matrix is in a traced extensive restriction category. This amounts to understanding the trace formulas for matrices of size
1+m\times 1+p
and
n+1\times n+1
respectively. We will explain how in either case, it reduces to computing traces of
2\times 2
matrices.

So let us start with a map
F:X+A_{1}+\ldots+A_{m}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X+B_{1}+\ldots+B_{p}
, which is an
1+m\times 1+p
matrix
F=[F_{i,j}]_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq 1+m\\
1\leq j\leq 1+p\end{subarray}}
, where
F_{1,1}:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X
,
F_{1,j+1}:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B_{j}
, and
F_{i+1,1}:A_{k}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X
, and
F_{i+1,j+1}:A_{i}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B_{j}
. As a short hand let us denote
\vec{A}=A_{1}+\ldots+A_{m}
and
\vec{B}=B_{1}+\ldots+B_{p}
. Then its trace
\mathsf{Tr}^{X}_{\vec{A},\vec{B}}(F):A_{1}+\ldots+A_{m}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B_{1}+\ldots+B_{p}
is a
m\times p
matrix whose components are given by
\iota_{i}\mathsf{Tr}^{X}_{\vec{A},\vec{B}}(F)\iota^{\circ}_{j}:A_{i}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B_{j}
. However by
[Tightening]
we get that
\iota_{i}\mathsf{Tr}^{X}_{\vec{A},\vec{B}}(F)\iota^{\circ}_{j}=\mathsf{Tr}^{X}_{A_{i},B_{j}}\left((1_{X}+\iota_{i})F(1_{X}+\iota^{\circ}_{j})\right)
. So expanding this out, we get that:

\mathsf{Tr}^{X}_{\vec{A},\vec{B}}(F)_{i,j}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}F_{1,1}&F_{1,j+1}\\
\hline\cr F_{i+1,1}&F_{i+1,j+1}\end{array}\right]

So as a matrix, the trace of
F
is written as:

\begin{gathered}\mathsf{Tr}^{X}_{\vec{A},\vec{B}}(F)\!=\!\left[\begin{array}[]{c|ccc}F_{1,1}&F_{1,2}&\ldots&F_{1,p+1}\\
\hline\cr F_{2,1}&F_{2,2}&\ldots&F_{2,p+1}\\
\vdots&\ldots&\ddots&\vdots\\
F_{m+1,1}&F_{m+1,2}&\ldots&F_{m+1,p+1}\end{array}\right]\!=\!\begin{bmatrix}\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}F_{1,1}&F_{1,2}\\
\hline\cr F_{2,1}&F_{2,2}\end{array}\right]&\ldots&\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}F_{1,1}&F_{1,p+1}\\
\hline\cr F_{2,1}&F_{2,p+1}\end{array}\right]\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}F_{1,1}&F_{1,2}\\
\hline\cr F_{m+1,1}&F_{m+1,p+1}\end{array}\right]&\ldots&\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1,1}&F_{1,p+1}\\
\hline\cr F_{m+1,1}&F_{m+1,p+1}\end{array}\right]\end{bmatrix}\end{gathered}

(75)

So the trace of an
1+m\times 1+p
matrix is the
m\times p
matrix whose components are traces of
2\times 2
matrices. As such, computing the trace of an
1+m\times 1+p
matrix is reduced to computing traces of
2\times 2
matrices.

Another way to see this is to write our map
X+A_{1}+\ldots+A_{m}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X+B_{1}+\ldots+B_{p}
as a block matrix
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}f&P\\
Q&R\end{smallmatrix}\right]
, where
f:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X
is an endomorphism,
P=\begin{bmatrix}P_{1}&\ldots&P_{p}\end{bmatrix}:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B_{1}+\ldots+B_{p}
is a
1\times p
row matrix,
Q=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}Q_{1}\\
\vdots\\
Q_{m}\end{smallmatrix}\right]:A_{1}+\ldots+A_{m}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X
a
m\times 1
column matrix, and
R=[R_{i,j}]:A_{1}+\ldots+A_{m}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B_{1}+\ldots+B_{p}
is an
m\times p
matrix. Then the traced matrix
\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}f&P\\
\hline\cr Q&R\end{array}\right]
is given by:

\displaystyle\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}f&P\\
\hline\cr Q&R\end{array}\right]_{i,j}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}f&P_{i}\\
\hline\cr Q_{j}&R_{i,j}\end{array}\right]

\displaystyle\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}f&P\\
\hline\cr Q&R\end{array}\right]=\begin{bmatrix}\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}f&P_{1}\\
\hline\cr Q_{1}&R_{1,1}\end{array}\right]&\ldots&\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}f&P_{p}\\
\hline\cr Q_{1}&R(1,p)\end{array}\right]\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}f&P_{1}\\
\hline\cr Q_{m}&R(m,1)\end{array}\right]&\ldots&\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}f&P_{p}\\
\hline\cr Q_{m}&R(m,p)\end{array}\right]\end{bmatrix}

On the other hand, consider now a map of type
X_{1}+\ldots+X_{n}+A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X_{1}+\ldots+X_{n}+B
, which is a
n+1\times n+1
matrix. Then successive applications of
[Vanishing]
tells us that tracing out
X_{1}+\ldots+X_{n}
is done by first tracing out
X_{1}
, then tracing out
X_{2}
, and so on (in fact
[Yanking]
also tells us that we can do it in any order). Then we may write down the higher order
[Vanishing]
inductively on
n
as follows. Consider a map
X_{1}+\ldots+X_{n+1}+A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X_{1}+\ldots+X_{n+1}+B
, which is an
n+2\times n+2
matrix, which we write down as a block matrix
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}f&T&U\\
V&S&g\\
W&h&k\end{smallmatrix}\right]
, where
S:X_{2}+\ldots+X_{n+1}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X_{2}+\ldots+X_{n+1}
is an
n\times n
matrix,
T:X_{2}+\ldots+X_{n}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X_{n+1}
and
U:X_{2}+\ldots+X_{n+1}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
are
n\times 1
column matrices,
V:X_{n+1}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X_{1}+\ldots+X_{n}
and
W:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X_{2}+\ldots+X_{n+1}
are
1\times n
row matrices, and the rest are maps
f:X_{1}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X_{1}
,
g:X_{1}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
,
h:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X_{1}
, and
k:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
. Then by
[Vanishing]
we have that:

\displaystyle\left[\begin{array}[]{cc|c}f&T&g\\
V&S&U\\
\hline\cr h&W&k\end{array}\right]=\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}f&T\\
\hline\cr V&S\end{array}\right]&\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}f&g\\
\hline\cr V&U\end{array}\right]\\
\\
\hline\cr\\
\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}f&T\\
\hline\cr h&W\end{array}\right]&\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}f&g\\
\hline\cr h&k\end{array}\right]\end{array}\right]

where on the left-hand side, the top left, top right, and bottom left inputs of the traced matrix can be reduced further using (
9
) and applying
[Vanishing]
again. Thus we see that the trace of an
n+1\times n+1
matrix is given by taking the trace of
2\times 2
matrices whose components are themselves
2\times 2
matrices, and so on. So again we have that computing the trace of a
n+1\times n+1
reduces to computing the trace of
2\times 2
matrices.

Finally, combining (
9
) and (
9
) together tells us that computing the trace of an
n+m\times n+p
matrix can be reduced to computing traces of
2\times 2
matrices. Therefore, for the matrix construction, it suffices to give a formula for
2\times 2
matrices and then extend it to
n+m\times n+p
matrices inductively using (
9
) and (
9
).

Proposition 9.15

Let
\mathbb{X}
be a finitely disjoint
\perp
-restriction category with a Kleene wand, so
(\mathbb{X},\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}})
is a
\perp
-itegory, then
\mathsf{MAT}\left[(\mathbb{X},\perp)\right]
is a traced extensive restriction category, where the trace operator,
\mathsf{Tr}
, is defined on a matrix
4
4

4

Recall that in
\mathsf{MAT}\left[(\mathbb{X},\perp)\right]
, the coproduct is given by concatenation of lists.

F:(X,A)=(X)+(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(X)+(B)=(X,B)
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathsf{Tr}^{(X)}_{(A),(B)}(F)=\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}f_{1,1}&f_{1,2}\\
\hline\cr f_{2,1}&f_{2,2}\end{array}\right]=\left[f_{2,2}\sqcup f_{2,1}\left(f_{1,1}\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}f_{1,2}\right)\right]

which we then extend to matrices of type
(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})+(A_{1},\ldots,A_{m})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})+(B_{1},\ldots,B_{m})
using (
9
) and (
9
). Furthermore,
{\mathcal{I}}:\mathbb{X}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{MAT}\left[(\mathbb{X},\perp)\right]
preserves the Kleene wands, that is,
{\mathcal{I}(f\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}g)}=\mathcal{I}(f)\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}\mathcal{I}(g)
.

Proof:
Prop
9.9
tells us that the proposed formula for the trace operator satisfies all the necessary axioms for
2\times 2
matrices. It is then not difficult to see (though very tedious to write down) that when extending to
n+m\times n+p
matrices using (
9
) and (
9
), the trace operator axioms all hold by definition and reducing it to the
2\times 2
case. Thus we get that
\mathsf{MAT}\left[(\mathbb{X},\perp)\right]
is indeed a traced extensive restriction category. Then by Prop
9.10
, we get a Kleene wand on
\mathsf{MAT}\left[(\mathbb{X},\perp)\right]
which can be expressed using the trace as in (
9.10
). Then using
[
\boldsymbol{\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-4.0pt\cr$|$\cr}}}}
.1]
, we compute that:

\mathcal{I}(f)\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}\mathcal{I}(g)=\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}\mathcal{I}(f)&\mathcal{I}(g)\\
\hline\cr\mathcal{I}(f)&\mathcal{I}(g)\end{array}\right]=\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}[f]&[g]\\
\hline\cr[f]&[g]\end{array}\right]=\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}f&g\\
\hline\cr f&g\end{array}\right]=\left[g\sqcup f(f\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}g)\right]=\left[f\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}g\right]=\mathcal{I}(f\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{
\hfil#\hfil\cr$\star$\cr\kern-3.87498pt\cr$|$\cr}}}g)

So
\mathcal{I}
preserves the Kleene wand as desired.

\Box

If the base interference restriction category has infinite disjoint joins, then we can give more explicit formulas for the Kleene wand and its induced uniform parametrized iteration operator and uniform trace operator.

Corollary 9.16

Let
\mathbb{X}
be a disjoint
\perp
-restriction category, then
\mathsf{MAT}\left[(\mathbb{X},\perp)\right]
is a traced extensive restriction category with inductive Kleene wand given as in Thm
5.16
, and induced uniform parametrized iteration operator and uniform trace operator defined as in Prop
9.12
.

Proof:
By Lemma
8.4
,
\mathsf{MAT}\left[(\mathbb{X},\perp)\right]
is also a strongly completely disjoint interference restriction category. Thus by Thm
5.16
, we get an inductive Kleene wand, whose induced parametrized iteration operator and trace operator are given as in Prop
9.12
.

\Box

Taking a step back, we can first use the disjoint join construction, and then apply the matrix construction to get a traced extensive restriction category.

Corollary 9.17

Let
\mathbb{X}
be a
\perp
-restriction category, then
\mathsf{MAT}\left[\mathsf{DJ}\left[(\mathbb{X},\perp)\right]\right]
is a traced extensive restriction category with inductive Kleene wand given as in Thm
5.16
, and induced uniform parametrized iteration operator and uniform trace operator defined as in Prop
9.12
.

Of course, we can simply start with any restriction category with restriction zeroes to build a traced extensive restriction category.

Corollary 9.18

Let
\mathbb{X}
be a restriction category with restriction zeroes, then
\mathsf{MAT}\left[\left(\mathsf{DJ}\left[(\mathbb{X},\perp_{0})\right],\perp_{0}\right)\right]
is a traced extensive restriction category with inductive Kleene wand given as in Thm
5.16
, and induced uniform parametrized iteration operator and uniform trace operator defined as in Prop
9.12
.
